Fig. 5
:

The streaming algorithm (in green) and difference estimators (in white/red) can be used to estimate the
F_{p}
value of the sliding window up to a small error caused by the elements in blue by subtracting the red estimates of the difference estimators from the estimate of the smallest suffix that contains the active elements in the sliding window.

Suffix-pivoted difference estimators.

A barrier in the sliding window model is that the difference estimator partitions the prefix of the stream rather than the suffix.
For example, compare the locations of the difference estimators between
Figure
3
and
Figure
5
.
In both the adversarially robust setting and the sliding window model, the difference estimator must approximate
F(v)-F(u)
for frequency vectors
v\succeq u
.
However in the robust setting,
u
is a fixed frequency vector induced by the prefix of the stream while in the sliding window model,
u
is a growing frequency vector induced by the suffix of the stream, where we recall that a prefix and suffix of length
t
are respectively defined to be the first and last
t
updates of a stream.
Thus, we define a
suffix-pivoted
difference estimator to handle the case where the vector
u
can change along with updates in the stream.
Fortunately, the fixed-prefix difference estimators that we have developed also function as suffix-pivoted difference estimators under this definition (we shall shortly demand a stronger definition).
We partition the stream to induce frequency vectors
v
and
u_{1}
so that
F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})\approx 2^{k}\cdot F(v+u_{1})
for a level
k
suffix-pivoted difference estimator.
We maintain
\beta
granularities, where
\beta
is roughly a logarithmic function of the smoothness parameter that is generally
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
.
We thus obtain an additive
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\beta}\right)\cdot F(v+u_{1})
error at each level
i\in[\beta]
, and summing the errors across all
\beta
granularities, gives
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{1})
total additive error, i.e.,
(1+\varepsilon)
multiplicative approximation to
F(v+u_{1})
.

Issues with dynamic differences.

There is a subtle but significant issue with naïvely applying the
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimators in the sliding window model.
Recall that a level
k
difference estimator guarantees an additive
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{1})
error to the difference
F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})

provided that

F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})\leq\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot F(v+u_{1})
.
In the sliding window model, additional updates to the stream may arrive and induce a frequency vector
u_{2}
for the same splitting time with
F(v+u_{2})-F(u_{2})\gg\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot F(v+u_{2})
, which is too large for the accuracy of a level
k
difference estimator.

Because of the smoothness parameter of an
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{q})
-smooth function
F
, we observe that if the difference
F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})
at some point is roughly
\varepsilon^{q}\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{1})
, then the difference
F(v+u_{2})-F(u_{2})\leq\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{2})
at
any
later point in the stream, regardless of subsequent updates.
Now the issue is that we maintain
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{q}}
difference estimators corresponding to additive error
\varepsilon^{q}\cdot F(v+u_{1})
, but since each of these differences can eventually be as large as
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{2})
, we potentially need higher accuracy for these differences, using space dependence
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
for each difference estimator.
Unfortunately, this results in space dependence
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{1+q}}
.

For example, consider the
F_{2}
moment function in
Figure
6
and suppose
v=(0,1)
and
u_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},0\right)
so that
F_{2}(v+u_{1})=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}+1
and
F_{2}(v+u_{1})-F_{2}(u_{1})=1\leq\varepsilon^{2}\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{1})
.
Hence, we have
\gamma=\varepsilon^{2}
and allocate space proportional to
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for the difference estimator for
v
.
We then have a number of updates so that
u_{2}=\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
.
Then
F_{2}(v+u_{2})=\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+1
and
F_{2}(v+u_{2})-F_{2}(u_{2})=\frac{2}{\varepsilon}+1\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{2})
for sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
.
Now we have
\gamma^{\prime}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, so we should allocate space proportional to
\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\approx\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
for the difference estimator for
v
, but we already only allocated space proportional to
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon}\approx 1
.
Therefore, we do not have any guarantee that our difference estimator will be accurate!
Moreover, there can be
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
such blocks
v
, so we cannot afford to allocate
\frac{\gamma^{\prime}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
space for each block, which would result in space dependency
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}
.

v
u_{1}
v
u_{2}

Fig. 6
:
For
v=(0,1)
,
u_{1}=\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},0\right)
, and
u_{2}=\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
, we have
F_{2}(v+u_{1})-F_{2}(u_{1})=\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right)\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{1})
but
F_{2}(v+u_{2})-F_{2}(u_{2})=\Omega(\varepsilon)\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{2})
, so it is unclear which granularity should be assigned to the difference estimator for
v
.

Stronger suffix-pivoted difference estimators.

We thus define the suffix-pivoted difference estimators to give an
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{2})
approximation to an
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{q})
-smooth function
F
, provided that
F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})\leq\varepsilon^{q}\cdot F(v+u_{1})
at
some point
in the stream with
u_{1}\preceq u_{2}
, even if
F(v+u_{2})-F(u_{2})=\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{2})
.
We give constructions of suffix-pivoted difference estimators under this stronger definition.
The main point is that we can now use roughly
2^{k}
difference estimators with space dependence
\frac{1}{2^{k}\varepsilon^{2}}
at level
k
for each
k
up to roughly
\beta=\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{q}}
, even if the difference eventually becomes a much larger fraction.
Moreover, the stronger guarantees of the suffix-pivoted difference estimators ensure that the additive error is still
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{2})
, even if the difference eventually becomes a much larger fraction of what it was originally. Also, additive error
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+u_{2})
is fine, because we only add up
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
difference estimators, and can rescale
\varepsilon
by a
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
factor.

This stronger definition may sound unachievable if
k=\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{q}}
since the space dependence is now
\frac{\varepsilon^{q}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, which is even smaller than
1
. But for an important
class of functions we consider, namely
F_{p}
-moment estimation, where
q=p
, this

\frac{\varepsilon^{p}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
multiplies a much larger term, namely,
n^{1-2/p}
, and so the space
is still much larger than
1
. Interestingly, we are able to beat the usual
n^{1-2/p}
space
lower bound for this problem by additionally removing global heavy hitters from all difference
estimators at a given level, which is a key idea.

In more detail, we give a suffix-pivoted difference estimator for
F_{p}
-moment estimation with
p>2
where the space dependency is
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
, which can still be much larger than
1
if
\gamma=\varepsilon^{p}
and
n
is large.
We still incur a
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}
space dependence due to running
\frac{1}{\gamma}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}
different difference estimators at level
k=\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}
, but this multiplies
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
which then becomes
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
, as desired.
As mentioned, we achieve space
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{p}}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
for each difference estimator at this level by running a single algorithm in parallel that uses
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right)
space to remove
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right)
global
L_{p}
heavy hitters in the sliding window, i.e., the elements
i\in[n]
with frequency
f_{i}^{p}\geq\varepsilon^{p}\cdot F_{p}
, and can be seen as a variance reduction technique.
Thus after the global heavy hitters are removed, the usual
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot n^{1-2/p}
lower bounds no longer apply.

Thus, we suffer an additive
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right)
term for finding heavy hitters, which is necessary of any algorithm, but it does not multiply the dominant
n^{1-2/p}
term in our space bound.
For more details but still a high level overview, see the construction details later in
Section
1.3
.
We thus obtain an additive
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\beta}\right)\cdot F(v)
error at each level, and summing the errors across all
\beta
levels gives
\varepsilon\cdot F(v)
total additive error, i.e.,
(1+\varepsilon)
multiplicative approximation to
F(v)
.

1.3
Difference Estimators

We now outline the ideas involved in developing our new difference estimators.
We describe our difference estimators for
F_{p}
-moment estimation, for both the adversarially robust and sliding window models; such estimators have applications to other streaming problems and can be used for estimating entropy and for finding the
L_{2}
-heavy hitters.
The high-level intuition for why difference estimators should be possible is that we leverage the fact that the difference is small into an estimator with significantly smaller variance.
Thus we can take the mean of a smaller number of independent estimators.

Difference estimators for
F_{2}
moment estimation.

We approximate
F_{2}(v)-F_{2}(u)
by noting that
F_{2}(v)=F_{2}((v-u)+u)=F_{2}(v-u)+2\langle v-u,u\rangle+F_{2}(u)
.
Thus if
F_{2}(v)-F_{2}(u)\leq\gamma F_{2}(u)
, then a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)
-approximation to
F_{2}(v)-F_{2}(u)
translates to additive error
\varepsilon\cdot F_{2}(u)
.
We then show that if streaming algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
approximation to
F_{2}(u)
and a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\right)
approximation to
F_{2}(v-u)
, then it gives an approximation to
\langle v-u,u\rangle
with additive error at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\cdot\|v-u\|_{2}\|u\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\cdot F_{2}(u)
, for
F_{2}(v-u)\leq\gamma\cdot F_{2}(u)
, where recall
F_{2}(u)=\|u\|_{2}^{2}
.
Hence
{\mathcal{B}}
can be directly built from the sketches maintained by
{\mathcal{A}}
in the case of
p=2
, with a space dependence of
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.

To adjust our construction to handle suffix-pivoted difference estimators, the crucial observation is that if
F_{2}(v+u_{1})-F_{2}(u_{1})=\varepsilon^{2}\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{1})
, then for any vector
u_{2}\succeq u_{1}
, we still have
F_{2}(v)\leq\varepsilon^{2}\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{2})
.
Thus if we decompose
F_{2}(v+u_{2})-F_{2}(u_{2})=F_{2}(v)+2\langle v,u_{2}\rangle
, then we can still obtain an additive
\varepsilon\cdot F_{2}(v+u_{2})
estimation to the difference by using a constant number of rows in the sketch to estimate
\langle v,u_{2}\rangle
because
F_{2}(v)
is so small.
Intuitively the main idea is that although the difference has increased from
F_{2}(v+u_{1})-F_{2}(u_{1})
to
F_{2}(v+u_{2})-F_{2}(u_{2})
, the difference between these differences is just the dot product
\langle v,u_{2}-u_{1}\rangle
and we can still use the same space to efficiently estimate the dot product because
\|v\|_{2}
remains the same.

Difference estimators for
F_{0}
estimation.

We remark that the
F_{0}
estimator of
[
BJK+02b
]
similarly maintains sketches that can be used to build a difference estimator for
F_{0}
with a space dependence of
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
The algorithm first subsamples each item in the universe with probability
\frac{1}{2^{k}}
at a level
k
so that the expected number of distinct items appearing throughout the stream at level
k
is
\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot F_{0}
.
The algorithm then determines a level
k
that contains
\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
distinct items throughout the stream, which the algorithm then rescales by a factor of
2^{k}
to obtain an unbiased estimator to
F_{0}
; it can be shown the variance of this estimator is roughly
\varepsilon^{2}\cdot(F_{0})^{2}
.

To obtain a difference estimator for
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)=\gamma F_{0}(u)
with
v\succeq u
, we instead count the number of items in a level with
\Theta\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
distinct items that appear in the prefix
u
of the stream.
It then follows that the expected number of the distinct items in
v
but not in
u
is
\Theta\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
.
Hence, we obtain a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)
-approximation to
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)
by first running the subsampling procedure on
u
and counting the number of distinct items at some level
k
with
\Theta\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
items.
We then run the same subsampling procedure with the same randomness on
v-u
by only counting the additional items that are occupied at level
k
, and rescaling this number by
2^{k}
. Note that additional items must only appear in
v
but not
u
, which is exactly
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)
.
Although level
k
does not necessarily give a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)
, it does give a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{\gamma}\right)
-approximation to
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)
, which translates to an additive
\varepsilon\cdot F_{0}(u)
approximation to
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)
since
F_{0}(v)-F_{0}(u)\leq\gamma F(u)
.

Difference estimator,
p\in(0,2)
.

Our difference estimators for
p\in(0,2)
are more involved, and we obtain an optimal dependence on
\log n
and
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
.
A natural starting point is the identity
F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u)=F_{p}(u+(v-u))-F_{p}(u)
and the expansion
F_{p}(u+(v-u))=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{p}{k}\cdot\langle u^{p-k},v^{k}\rangle
, where
v^{k}
denotes the coordinate-wise exponent of
u
.
We would like to approximate each term in the series to approximate the overall difference.
However, this approach fails for non-integer
p<2
, since coordinates of the frequency vector
u
may be zero, so that
u^{p-k}
is undefined for
k=2
, and there does not seem to be a simple fix.

Another natural approach is the
L_{p}
strong tracking algorithm of
[
BDN17
]
, which generates
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
random vectors
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots
of
p
-stable random variables.
The estimate of
L_{p}(v)
is then the median of the absolute values of the inner products
\langle z_{1},v\rangle,\langle z_{2},v\rangle,\ldots
.
However, it seems challenging to recover the difference
F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u)
from the inner products
\langle z_{1},v\rangle,\langle z_{2},v\rangle,\ldots
and
\langle z_{1},u\rangle,\langle z_{2},u\rangle,\ldots
.

Instead, we consider a variant of Li’s geometric mean estimator
[
Li08
]
.
As in the strong
L_{p}
-tracker of
[
BDN17
]
, we generate
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
random vectors
z_{1},z_{2},\ldots
of
p
-stable variables.
For a fixed positive integer constant
q
, we form
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
so that each
y_{i}
is the geometric mean of the absolute values of
q
consecutive inner products
\langle z_{1+(i-1)q},v\rangle,\ldots,\langle z_{iq},v\rangle
.
The final output is the average of the estimators
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
.
The key property of Li’s geometric mean estimator is that
Z:=\prod_{j=1+(i-1)q}^{iq}|\langle z_{j},v\rangle|^{p/q}-\prod_{j=1+(i-1)q}^{iq}|\langle z_{j},u\rangle|^{p/q}
is an unbiased estimate to
F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u)
whose behavior we can analyze.
Namely, we relate
Z
to
\prod_{j=1+(i-1)q}^{iq}\left(1+\left(\frac{|\langle z_{j},v-u\rangle|}{|\langle z_{j},u\rangle|}\right)^{p/q}\right)
, which has small variance given
\frac{F_{p}(v-u)}{F_{p}(u)}\leq\gamma
.
Thus we require a smaller number of independent estimators and obtain a more efficient difference estimator for
F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u)
for fixed
u
and
v
with constant probability.

To achieve an optimal dependence on
\log n
, we cannot afford to union bound over
\poly(n)
possible frequency vectors
u
.
Instead we use an approach similar to the strong trackers of
[
BCIW16
,
BCI+17
,
BDN17
]
.
We set a constant
q
and split the stream into roughly
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{q/p}}
times
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots
between which the difference estimator increases by
\varepsilon^{q/p}\cdot(F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u))
and apply a union bound to argue correctness at these times
\{r_{i}\}
, incurring a
\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
term.
To bound the difference estimator between times
r_{i}
and
r_{i+1}
for a fixed
i
, we note that the difference estimator only increases by
\varepsilon^{q/p}\cdot(F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u))
from
r_{i}
to
r_{i+1}
, so that we still obtain a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u)
with a
\varepsilon^{1-q/p}
-approximation to the difference.
We then bound the probability that the supremum of the error between times
r_{i}
and
r_{i+1}
is bounded by
\varepsilon^{1-q/p}
by applying known results from chaining
[
BCIW16
,
BCI+17
,
BDN17
]
, thus avoiding additional
\log n
factors.
To derandomize our algorithm, we give a generalization of a pseudorandom generator that fools half-space queries shown by
[
GKM18
,
JW18
]
.
Our result shows a high-probability derandomization of any algorithm that stores the product of a number of frequency vectors induced by the stream between fixed times, with a matrix of i.i.d. entries.
We then union bound over all possible stopping times to argue the correctness guarantees of our difference estimator still hold under the derandomization.
Interestingly, we do not guarantee the same output distribution as that of using independent
p
-stable random variables, yet we still have a correct algorithm in low memory.
We also show that Li’s geometric estimator can be used as a strong tracker for
L_{p}
.

Our construction for the suffix-pivoted difference estimator is the same.
We note that upon a change of notation, the key property to efficiently estimate
F_{p}(v+u_{1})-F_{p}(u_{1})
is that
F_{p}(v)
is small.
Moreover, we can express the variance of Li’s geometric estimator in terms of
F_{p}(v)
so that smaller values of
F_{p}(v)
correspond to smaller variance for the estimators and thus we can use a smaller number of independent copies.
For the suffix-pivoted difference estimator, it may be true that
F_{p}(v+u_{2})-F_{p}(u_{2})
could be much larger than
F_{p}(v+u_{1})-F_{p}(u_{1})
for some
u_{2}\succeq u_{1}
, but
F_{p}(v)
will still be “small” provided that
F_{p}(v+u_{1})-F_{p}(u_{1})
is “small”.
Hence, the variance for Li’s geometric estimator will still be small despite the difference potentially being much larger.

Fixed-prefix difference estimator,
p>2
.

Our difference estimator
{\mathcal{B}}
for
F_{p}
for integers
p>2
does use the expansion
F_{p}(u+(v-u))-F_{p}(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\binom{p}{k}\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
, where
u^{k}
denotes the coordinate-wise
k
-th power of
u
.
To obtain an unbiased estimator of
\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
, suppose we sample a coordinate
a\in[n]
with probability
\frac{u_{a}^{k}}{\|u\|_{k}^{k}}
and set
Z
to be the
a
-th coordinate of
(v-u)^{p-k}
, which we can explicitly track since the updates to the frequency vector
v-u
arrive in the stream after the updates to
u
.
If we could obtain an unbiased estimate
Y
to
\|u\|_{k}^{k}
, then the expected value of
YZ
would be exactly
\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
.
One could try to output a coordinate
a\in[n]
with probability
(1\pm\varepsilon)\frac{u_{a}^{k}}{\|u\|_{k}^{k}}
.
Such “approximate”
L_{p}
-samplers exist for
p\leq 2

[
MW10
,
JST11
,
JW18
]
, though this would lead to an overall suboptimal dependence on
\varepsilon
.
We could also potentially use an efficient “perfect”
L_{p}
-sampler, which outputs a coordinate
a\in[n]
with probability
\frac{u_{a}^{p}}{\|u\|_{p}^{p}}
.
Perfect
L_{p}
-samplers are known for
p\leq 2

[
JW18
]
.
Corresponding samplers for
p>2
are currently unknown, though it may be possible to extend the
L_{p}
samplers of
[
JW18
]
to
p>2
and potentially obtain a difference estimator algorithm.
Instead, we use the perfect
L_{2}
-sampler of
[
JW18
]
to return a coordinate
a\in[n]
with probability
\frac{u_{a}^{2}}{\|u\|_{2}^{2}}\pm\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
.
We also obtain unbiased estimates
X
and
Y
of
u_{a}^{k-2}
and
\|u\|_{2}^{2}
with low variance, respectively, by using a
CountSketch
algorithm and our
F_{2}
moment estimation algorithm.
Given
a\in[n]
, we then track the
a
-th coordinate of
(v-u)^{p-k}
exactly.
We show that the product of these terms
X
,
Y
, and
(v-u)^{p-k}
forms an unbiased estimate to
\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
.
Unfortunately, the variance of these estimates is still too large.
We thus also find approximate frequencies to the heavy-hitters of
u
, i.e., we estimate each
u_{a}
such that
u_{a}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{16\gamma^{1-1/p}}\|u\|_{p}
and track the corresponding
a
-th coordinate of
(v-u)^{p-k}
exactly.
We remove these terms before we perform the perfect
L_{2}
sampling, so that the resulting unbiased estimate to
\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
has smaller variance.
We then show that taking the mean of enough repetitions gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
.
By repeating the estimator for each summand in
\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}\binom{p}{k}\langle u^{k},(v-u)^{p-k}\rangle
, it follows that we obtain a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator for
F_{p}
.

Suffix-pivoted difference estimator,
p>2
.

Our construction for the suffix-pivoted difference estimator requires a bit more care.
We can again write
F_{p}(v)-F_{p}(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle(v-u)^{p-k},u^{k}\rangle
.
However, we require
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right)
difference estimators due to the smoothness parameter of
F_{p}
.
Namely, we implement suffix-pivoted difference estimators with
\gamma
as small as
\varepsilon^{p}
whereas we previously implemented prefix-fixed difference estimators with
\gamma
roughly as small as
\varepsilon^{2}
.
Thus, we can no longer necessarily run a heavy-hitter algorithm that uses space proportional to
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2-2/p}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
for each block of the stream corresponding to a difference estimator, since the quantity is no longer well-defined for
\gamma=\varepsilon^{p}
.

On the other hand, the necessity to run separate heavy-hitter algorithms for each block in the fixed-prefix difference estimator originated from the adversarial robustness requiring fresh randomness in each block.
The sliding window model requires no such restriction and thus we instead we run a single heavy-hitter algorithm in parallel that uses space
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
and
simultaneously
finds a list
{\mathcal{H}}
of all heavy-hitters in the sliding window.
In particular,
{\mathcal{H}}
will report an approximate frequency of each heavy-hitter in each block assigned to a difference estimator.
The main point is that
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
space is used to identify the heavy-hitters but only
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}\log n\right)
space is used to track their approximate frequency in each block.
Hence, the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{p}}
dependency is still present but it now multiplies a lower-order term.
We can then decompose the difference
\sum_{k=1}^{p}\langle(v-u)^{p-k},u^{k}\rangle
into the amount contributed by the heavy items
\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}(v_{a}-u_{a})^{p-k}u_{a}^{k}
and the amount contributed by the items that are not heavy
\sum_{a\notin{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}(v_{a}-u_{a})^{p-k}u_{a}^{k}
.
We show that to estimate
\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}(v_{a}-u_{a})^{p-k}u_{a}^{k}
, it again suffices to estimate the value of
v_{a}-u_{a}
and then subsequently read off the corresponding coordinate
u_{a}
.

It remains to estimate
\sum_{a\notin{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}(v_{a}-u_{a})^{p-k}u_{a}^{k}
.
We use the same approach of perfect
L_{2}
sampling coordinates from
v-u
and reading off the corresponding coordinates in
u
, so that each perfect
L_{2}
sample and the corresponding coordinate in
u
forms an estimate of the inner product.
Crucially we can use a more refined level set analysis to upper bound the variance of the inner product estimate by
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)\cdot(F_{p}(v))^{2}
, even when
\gamma
is as small as
\varepsilon^{p}
.
Thus as before, smaller values of
F_{p}(v)
correspond to smaller variance for the estimators and thus we can use a smaller number of independent copies of the inner product estimates.
Since each inner product estimate requires polylogarithmic space, it suffices to take the mean of
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}n^{1-2/p}\right)
such inner product estimators to form our difference estimator.

Heavy hitters.

Our framework extends to an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for finding
L_{2}
heavy hitters.
We first run the same
F_{2}
moment estimation algorithm that stitches together sketches of different granularities, which partitions the stream of length
m
is partitioned into
\beta
blocks.
Observe that a heavy-hitter
i\in[n]
with
f_{i}>\varepsilon\cdot L_{2}(1,m)
must have frequency at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{\beta}\cdot L_{2}(1,m)
in one of these
\beta
blocks.
Now if
F_{2}(t_{j},t_{j+1})=\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot F_{2}(1,m)
for a block corresponding to time interval
[t_{j},t_{j+1}]
in which
i
has frequency at least
\frac{\varepsilon}{\beta}\cdot L_{2}(1,m)
, then
f_{i}^{2}\geq\frac{2^{k}\varepsilon^{2}}{\beta^{2}}F_{2}(t_{j},t_{j+1})
.
We can identify
i
using a much higher threshold and thus much less space, i.e., space proportional to
\frac{\beta^{2}}{2^{k}\varepsilon^{2}}
rather than
\frac{\beta^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
.
Thus in parallel with our
F_{2}
moment estimation algorithm, each time we run a difference estimator
{\mathcal{B}}
and an
F_{2}
-estimation algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
with a specific granularity, we also run a heavy hitters algorithm with threshold corresponding to the same granularity of
{\mathcal{B}}
.
For example, if the difference estimator on a block contributes
\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot F_{2}(1,m)
to the overall
F_{2}
moment, then we use a level
k
difference estimator
{\mathcal{B}}
with effective accuracy
1+\frac{2^{k}\varepsilon}{\beta}
and we similarly run a heavy hitters algorithm to detect items that are
\frac{2^{k}\varepsilon^{2}}{\beta^{2}}
-heavy with respect to
F_{2}
on the block.
We then explicitly count each reported item to remove all items that may be heavy with respect to a single block, but not the overall stream, and obtain an accurate count for each heavy hitter.

1.4
Preliminaries

We use the notation
[n]
to denote the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
for any positive integer
n
.
We use
\poly(n)
to denote a constant degree polynomial in
n
.
When an event has probability
1-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
of occurring, we say the event occurs with high probability.
We use
\polylog(n)
to denote a polynomial in
\log n
.

For a function
F
, let
F(t_{1},t_{2})
denote the value of
F
on the underlying frequency vector induced between times
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, inclusive.
In the basic insertion-only stream, the
t
-th stream update
u_{t}
is an integer in
[n]
, where
t\in[m]
for a stream of length
m
.
The updates implicitly define a frequency vector
f\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that the effect of each update is to increase the value of a coordinate of
f
.
Thus, we have
f_{i}=|\{t\,:\,u_{t}=i\}|
for each
i\in[n]=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
In the sliding window model, the frequency vector is induced by only the most recent
W
updates in an insertion-only stream.
Hence if
W\geq m
for a stream of length
m
, the model reduces to the insertion-only streaming model and if
W<m
, then
f_{i}=|\{t\,:\,u_{t}=i\textrm{ and }t\geq m-W+1\}|
for each
i\in[n]
.
In turnstile streams, each update
u_{t}=(\Delta_{t},i_{t})
, where
\Delta_{t}\in\{-1,+1\}
and
i_{t}\in[n]
.
Hence, each update either increases or decreases a coordinate of
f
.
For vectors
u
and
v
of length
n
, we use the notation
u\succeq v
to denote that
u
coordinate-wise dominates
v
, i.e.,
u_{i}\geq v_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
.

Let
\lsb(x,i)
be the
i
-th least significant bit in the binary representation of
x
, when using
1
-indexing, e.g., we start counting indices from
1
rather than
0
.
Let
\numbits(x)
be the number of nonzero bits in the binary representation of
x
.
For example,
\lsb(6,1)=2
,
\lsb(6,2)=3
, and
\numbits(x)=2
, since
6=110_{2}
.
As another example,
\lsb(8,1)=4
and
\numbits(x)=1
, since
8=1000_{2}
.

Frequency moments.

The space complexity of approximating the frequency moments
F_{p}
is one of the oldest and most fundamental problems on streams, originating with the work of Alon, Matias, and Szegedy
[
AMS99
]
.
For example, the
F_{2}
-estimation problem corresponds to the size of a self-join
[
AMS99
]
and tracking it with limited storage is essential
[
AGMS02
]
.
Given
p,\varepsilon>0
, the
p
-th frequency moment estimation problem is to approximate
F_{p}=\sum_{i\in[n]}f_{i}^{p}
within a
(1\pm\varepsilon)
factor for a frequency vector
f\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
The complexity of this problem and the related
L_{p}
-norm estimation problem differs greatly for different values of
p
.
For
p>2
,
[
BJKS04
,
CKS03
]
show that for the streaming model, the memory required for
F_{p}
estimation requires polynomial factors in
n
, whereas polylogarithmic space is achievable for
p\leq 2

[
AMS99
,
Ind06
,
Li08
,
KNW10a
,
KNPW11
,
BDN17
]
.
We note that
F_{p}
estimation for fractional
p
near
1
is used for empirical entropy estimation
[
HNO08
]
, and
p=0.25
and
p=0.5
are useful for mining tabular data
[
CIKM02
]
.

L_{1}
-norm estimation is used in network traffic monitoring
[
FKSV02
]
, low-rank approximation and linear regression
[
FMSW10
]
, dynamic earth-mover distance approximation
[
Ind04
]
, and cascaded norm estimation
[
JW09
]
.

L_{2}
-norm estimation is used for estimating join and self-join sizes
[
AGMS02
]
, randomized numerical linear algebra
[
CW09b
]
, and for detecting network anomalies
[
KSZC03
,
TZ04
]
.
The values
p=3
and
p=4
also respectively correspond to the skewness and the kurtosis of a stream
[
AMS99
]
.

For a frequency vector
f
with length
n
, we define
F_{p}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|f_{i}|^{p}
.
The
L_{p}
norm
2
2

2

Observe that
L_{p}
does not satisfy the triangle inequality for
0<p<1
and thus is not a norm, but is still a well-defined quantity.
of
f
is defined by
L_{p}(f)=(F_{p}(f))^{1/p}
.
We also use the notation
\|f\|_{p}
to denote the
L_{p}
norm of
f
.
The
F_{p}
-moment estimation problem is also often stated as the norm estimation problem, since an algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to one of these problems can be modified to output a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to the other using a constant rescaling of
\varepsilon
(for constant
p
); thus we use these two equivalent problems interchangeably.
The
L_{2}
-heavy hitters problem is to output all coordinates
i
such that
f_{i}\geq\varepsilon\cdot L_{2}
.
The problem permits the output of coordinates
j
with
f_{j}\leq\varepsilon\cdot L_{2}
provided that
f_{j}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot L_{2}
.
Moreover, each coordinate output by the algorithm also requires an estimate to its frequency, with additive error at most
\frac{1}{2}\cdot L_{2}
.

Definition 1.9

(
(\varepsilon,m)
-flip number)
.

[
BJWY20
]

For
\varepsilon>0
and an integer
m>0
, the
(\varepsilon,m)
-flip number of a sequence
y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}
is the largest integer
k
such that there exist
0\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq m
with
y_{i_{j-1}}\notin[(1-\varepsilon)y_{i_{j}},(1+\varepsilon)y_{i_{j}}]
for all integers
j\in[2,k]
.

Definition 1.10

(
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number)
.

For
\varepsilon>0
and an integer
m>0
, the
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number for a non-negative function
F
on a stream of updates
1=u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
is the largest integer
k
such that there exist
0\leq i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq m
with for each
j\in[2,k]
, either
F(u_{1}:u_{j-1})\notin[(1-\varepsilon)F(u_{1}:u_{j}),(1+\varepsilon)F(u_{1}:u_{j})]
or
F(u_{j-1}:u_{j})\geq\varepsilon F(1:u_{j-1})
.

Definition 1.11

(Strong tracking)
.

Let
f^{(1)},\ldots,f^{(m)}
be the frequency vectors induced by a stream of length
m
and let
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function on frequency vectors.
An algorithm
A
provides
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-strong
g
-tracking if at each time step
t\in[m]
,
A
outputs an estimate
X_{t}
such that

|X_{t}-g(f^{(t)})|\leq\varepsilon|g(f^{(t)})|

for all
t\in[m]
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Observation 1.12
.

[
BJWY20
]

For
p>0
, the
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number of
\|x\|_{p}^{p}
in the insertion-only model is
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log m\right)
for
p\leq 2
and
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{p}{\varepsilon}\log m\right)
for
p>2
.

2
Framework for Adversarially Robust Streaming Algorithms

In this section, we describe a general framework for adversarially robust streaming algorithms, using the sketch stitching and granularity changing techniques.
We first require the following specific form of a difference estimator.

Definition 2.1

(Fixed-Prefix Difference Estimator)
.

Given a stream
{\mathcal{S}}
, a fixed time
t_{1}
, and a splitting time
t_{2}
that is only revealed at time
t_{2}
, let frequency vector
v
be induced by the updates of
{\mathcal{S}}
from time
t_{1}
to
t_{2}
and frequency vector
w_{t}
be induced by updates from time
t_{2}
to
t
exclusive.
Given an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon>0
and a failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
, a streaming algorithm
{\mathcal{B}}(t_{1},t_{2},t,\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
is a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-
difference estimator
for a function
F
if, with probability at least
1-\delta
, it outputs an additive
\varepsilon\cdot F(v)
approximation to
F(v+w_{t})-F(v)
simultaneously for all
t\geq t_{2}
with
F(v+w_{t})-F(v)\leq\gamma\cdot F(v)
and
F(w_{t})\leq\gamma F(v)
for a ratio parameter
\gamma\in(0,1]
.

We shall use the shorthand “difference estimator” terminology to refer to
Definition
1.1
until
Section
8
; in
Section
8
we will introduce an additional notion of a difference estimator for when
F(v)
can change.

It is easy to see that difference estimators offer an immediate means to approximation algorithms.
See
1.2

Proof.

Since
F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i})=F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i-1})+(F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i-1})-F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i}))
and the difference is bounded by
\gamma_{i}\cdot F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i-1})
by the definition of difference estimator, then we have the invariant
F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i})\leq(1+\gamma_{i})F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i-1})\leq 2F(u)
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
by induction.
Thus the sum of the additive errors from each difference estimator is at most
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}\cdot F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i-1})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot 2F(u)=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(u)
.
Similarly the additive error due to the
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)
-approximation to
F(u)
is also
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(u)
, so that by monotonicity of
F
and a telescoping argument the sum gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
multiplicative approximation to
F(u+v_{1}+\ldots+v_{i})
.
Since each difference estimator fails with probability at most
\frac{1}{3k}
, then by a union bound, the total probability of failure is at most
\frac{1}{3}
.
∎

See
1.3

Proof.

The total space is
\sum_{i=1}^{k}N_{i}\cdot\frac{\gamma_{i}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S
.
Since
N_{i}\leq 2^{i}
,
\gamma_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2^{i}}
, and
k=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
, then the total space is at most
k\cdot\frac{S}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq\mathcal{O}\left(S\log n\right){\varepsilon^{2}}
.
∎

2.1
Algorithm

We first describe a simplified version of our adversarially robust framework that adapts the usage of difference estimators.
To achieve a robust
(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right))
-approximation,
[
BJWY20
]
used a “switch-a-sketch” technique that maintains
(\varepsilon,m)
-flip number
\xi
(recall
Definition
1.9
) independent subroutines that each provide a
(1+\varepsilon)
to a function
F
evaluated on the frequency vector induced by the stream, with high probability.
For the remainder of the discussion, we assume the
\xi=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
, which is true for many important functions
F
, especially the important
F_{p}
moments and moreover, that the twist number
\lambda=\xi
, which is true for insertion-only streams.
To prevent an adversary from affecting the output of the algorithm, each subroutine is effectively only used once.
The output of the
i
-th subroutine is only used the first time the true output of the subroutine is at least
(1+\varepsilon)^{i}
.
The algorithm of
[
BJWY20
]
then repeatedly outputs this value until the
(i+1)
-st subroutine is at least
(1+\varepsilon)^{i+1}
, at which point the algorithm switches to using the output of the
(i+1)
-st subroutine instead.
Hence, the adversary information-theoretically knows nothing about the internal randomness of the
i
-th subroutine until the output is at least
(1+\varepsilon)^{i}
.
However, due to monotonicity of
F
and correctness of the oblivious
(i+1)
-st instance, whatever knowledge the adversary gains about the
i
-th instance does not impact the internal randomness of future instances.
Intuitively, the switch-a-sketch approach uses a sketch once and switches to another sketch once the estimated
F
has increased by
(1+\varepsilon)
.

F
can only increase
\lambda
times by definition of the
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number.
Since
\lambda=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
, this approach generally achieves
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3}}
space dependency.

Algorithm 1

Framework for Robust Algorithms on Insertion-Only Streams

1:

Stream
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in[n]
of updates to coordinates of an underlying frequency vector, accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator
{\mathcal{B}}
for
F
with space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for
C\geq 1
, oblivious strong tracker
{\mathcal{A}}
for
F

Robust

-approximation to
F

\delta\leftarrow\frac{1}{\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)}
,
\zeta\leftarrow\frac{2}{2^{(C-1)/4}-1}
,
\eta\leftarrow\frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}
,
\beta\leftarrow{\left\lceil\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}

4:

,
\varphi\leftarrow 2^{(C-1)/4}
,
\gamma_{j}\leftarrow 2^{j-1}\eta

For
j\in[\beta]
,
\eta_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\eta}{\beta}
if
C=1
,
\eta_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-j}}
if
C>1
.

\triangleright
Accuracy for each difference estimator

for

u_{t}\in[n]
,
t\in[m]

do

7:

X\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{a+1}(1,t,\eta,\delta)

8:

if

X>2^{a}

then

\triangleright
Switch sketch at top layer

,

,
Z_{a}\leftarrow X
,

for
j\in[\beta]
.

X\leftarrow{\textsc{EstimateF}}

\triangleright

X
for

if

\triangleright
Switch sketch at lower layer

b\leftarrow b+1
,

,
j\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{b}{2^{k}}\right\rfloor}

Z_{a,k}\leftarrow{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta)

\triangleright
Freeze old sketch

14:

t_{a,j}\leftarrow t
for
j\in[k]
.

\triangleright
Update difference estimator times

15:

return

\left(1+\frac{b\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{a}

\triangleright
Output estimate for round
t

Subroutine
EstimateF
of

1

1:

X\leftarrow Z_{a}
,
k\leftarrow\numbits(b+1)
,
z_{i}\leftarrow\lsb(b+1,k+1-i)
for
i\in[k]
.

2:

\triangleright

z_{1}>\ldots>z_{k}
are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of
b+1
.

3:

for

1\leq j\leq k-1

do

\triangleright
Compile previous frozen components for estimator
X

4:

X\leftarrow X+Z_{a,j}

5:

j\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{b+1}{2^{z_{k}}}\right\rfloor}

\triangleright
Use unrevealed sketch for last component

7:

return

X

We first observe that if we instead use the switch-a-sketch technique each time
F
increases by a power of
2
, then we only need to switch
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
sketches.
Effectively, this follows from setting
\varepsilon=\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
in the value of
\lambda
.
Let
t_{i}
be the first time
F
of the stream surpasses
2^{i}
.
The challenge is then achieving a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F
at the times between each
2^{i}
and
2^{i+1}
.
Let
u
be the underlying frequency vector at time
t_{i}
, so that
F(u)\geq 2^{i}
.
If
v
is the underlying frequency vector at some time between
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
, then we can decompose
F(v)=F(u)+\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}(F(u_{j})-F(u_{j-1}))
, where we use the convention that
u_{0}=u
and
u_{\beta}=j
.
Moreover, we assume that
F(u_{j})-F(u_{j-1})\leq\gamma\cdot F(v)
for
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2^{j}}
.

Our key observation is that because we only care about a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F(v)
, we do not need a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to each of the differences
F(u_{j})-F(u_{j-1})
, which may be significantly smaller than
F(v)
.
For example, note that a
(2^{j}\cdot\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F(u_{j})-F(u_{j-1})
only equates to an additive
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\cdot F(v)\right)
error, since
F(u_{j})-F(u_{j-1})=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{2^{j}}\right)\cdot F(v)
.
We require
\frac{1}{\gamma}
instances of algorithms with such accuracies, to account for the various possible vectors
v
.
Thus if there exists an algorithm that uses
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}S(n)
bits of space to output additive
\varepsilon\cdot F(v)
error, then the space required across the level
j
estimators is
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}S(n)
bits of space.
Since there are at most
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
levels, then we do not incur any additional factors in
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
.
Recall that a difference estimator (
Definition
1.1
) to
F
serves exactly this purpose!

It is not obvious how to obtain a difference estimator for various functions
F
.
However, for the purposes of a general framework, the theoretical assumption of such a quantity suffices; we shall give explicit difference estimators for specific functions
F
of interest.
The framework appears in full in
Algorithm
1
.

Interpretation of
Algorithm
1
.

We now translate between the previous intuition and the pseudocode of
Algorithm
1
.
The streaming algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}_{a+1}
attempts to notify the algorithm whenever the value of
F
on the underlying frequency vector induced by the stream has increased by a power of two.
The role of
a
serves as a counter, ensuring that a new instance of
{\mathcal{A}}
is used each time the value of
a
has increased.
Since
a
increases when
{\mathcal{A}}_{a+1}>2^{a}
, i.e., the value of
F
has increased by roughly a factor of two, then the instances
{\mathcal{A}}
, indexed by
a
, perform sketch switching with the finest accuracy.
Thus,
Algorithm
1
defines
Z
to be the value of
{\mathcal{A}}_{a}
when it is revealed and never uses
{\mathcal{A}}_{a}
again.

It remains for
Algorithm
1
to stitch the sketches between the times when
a
increases.
Suppose
t_{a}
is the time when
Z
was most recently defined.
Then
Algorithm
1
uses the variables
Z_{a,k}
to stitch together the sketches of the difference estimators
{\mathcal{B}}
for
F(1:t)-F(1:t_{a})
.
Each variable
Z_{a,k}
refers to a separate granularity of the difference estimator
{\mathcal{B}}
.
Like the streaming algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
, the difference estimators
{\mathcal{B}}
are indexed by the counter
a
to distinguish between the instances required for the sketch switching technique.
Additionally, the difference estimators
{\mathcal{B}}
for the variable
Z_{a,k}
are indexed by a counter
j
that represents the number of times a level
k
difference estimator is required throughout the times
t
that we roughly have
2^{a}<F(1:t)<2^{a+1}
.
The subroutine
EstimateF
then stitches together the values
Z_{a,k}
to form an estimate
X
of
F(1:t)
.
Similarly,
Algorithm
1
stitches together the values
Z_{a,k}
to form an estimate
X
of
F(1:t)
, which it then rounds as the output.
The actual stitching process requires partitioning
F(1:t)-F(1:t_{a})
, so that the appropriate granularities may be used for each difference estimator.
This is monitored by the nonzero bits in the binary representation of the counter
b
, which roughly denotes the ratio of
F(1:t)-F(1:t_{a})
to
F(1:t_{a})
.
Now of course an inconvenience in the actual analysis is that because
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}
are approximation algorithms, then we do not exactly have
2^{a}<F(1:t)<2^{a+1}
, although this is true up to a multiplicative
(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right))
-factor.

2.2
Analysis

We first show correctness of
Algorithm
1
at times where the counter
a
increases.
This allows us to subsequently focus on times
t\in(t_{i},t_{i+1})
between points in the stream where the counter increases.

Lemma 2.2

(Correctness when
F
doubles)
.

Let
F
be a monotonic function with
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number
\lambda=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
for
\log m=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
.
For any integer
i>0
, let
t_{i}
be the update time during which the counter
a
in
Algorithm
1
is first set to
i
.
Then
Algorithm
1
outputs a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)
-approximation to
F
at all times
t_{i}
, with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
.

Proof.

Note that by definition, the counter
a
in
Algorithm
1
is incremented from
i-1
to
i
in round
t_{i}
.
Thus all previous outputs of
Algorithm
1
have not used the subroutine
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.
Hence, the adversarial input is independent of the randomness of
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.
Thus by the correctness guarantee of the algorithm,
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
outputs a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)
-approximation to
F
at time
t_{i}
, with probability
1-\delta
.
Since
F
has twist number
\lambda=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
, then there are at most
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
update times
t_{i}
in which the counter
a
is increased.
By a union bound,
Algorithm
1
outputs a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)
-approximation to
F
at all times
t_{i}
, with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
.
∎

For any integer
i>0
, let
t_{i}
be the update time during which the counter
a
in
Algorithm
1
is first set to
i
.
Since
Lemma
2.2
shows correctness at all times
t_{i}
, it remains to analyze the behavior of
Algorithm
1
between each of the times
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
.

Lemma 2.3

(Geometric bounds on splitting times)
.

For each integer
j\geq 0
and a fixed integer
i
, let
u_{i,j}
be the last round for which
Z_{i,j}
in
Algorithm
1
is defined.
With probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
, we have
F(1,u_{i,j})-F(1,t_{i,j})\leq\frac{2^{j}\varepsilon}{64}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
for each positive integer
j>1
.

Proof.

As before, let
t_{i}
be the update time during which the counter
a
in
Algorithm
1
is first set to
i
for any integer
i>0
.
Let
t_{i,j}
be the first round such that the counter
b
in
Algorithm
1
is first set to
j
, and we use the convention that
t_{i,0}=t_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that
Algorithm
1
outputs a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)
-approximation to the value of
F
at all times
t_{i}
, so that by
Lemma
2.2
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
.
For the remainder of the proof, we fix the integer
i
and show correctness between
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
.
Note that conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have correctness at times
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event that the first
\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
instances of
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}
in
Algorithm
1
are correct, so that
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]\geq 1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
by a union bound.
We show by induction on
b
that conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we have that
F(1,u_{i,j})-F(1,t_{i,j})\leq\frac{1}{2^{\beta-j-3}}F(1,t_{i})
for
j\geq 1
.

For the base case
b=1
, only
Z_{i,1}
is defined. Suppose by way of contradiction that
F(1,u_{i,1})-F(1,t_{i,1})>\frac{1}{2^{\beta-j-3}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
.
Since
\beta={\left\lceil\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}
, then
F(1,u_{i,1})-F(1,t_{i,1})>\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
.
Observe that
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,1}(1,t_{i,1},u_{i,1},\gamma_{1},\eta_{1},\delta)
gives an
\eta_{1}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
additive approximation to
F(1,u_{i,1})-F(1,t_{i,1})
.
Hence, the estimate
Z_{i,1}
of
F(1,u_{i,1})-F(1,t_{i,1})
from
EstimateF
is at least

Z_{i,1}\geq F(1,u_{i,1})-F(1,t_{i,1})-\eta_{1}\cdot F(1,t_{i})>\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\cdot F(1,t_{i}),

since
\eta_{1}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-1}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
for
\eta=\frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}
and
\varphi>1
.
But this contradicts the fact that the counter
b
is set to
j=1
, since

Z_{i}+Z_{i,1}>\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)Z_{i},

since
Z_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot F(1,t_{i})
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
.
Thus we have
F(1,u_{i,1})-F(1,t_{i,1})>\frac{1}{2^{\beta-j-3}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
, which completes the base case
j=1
.

For a fixed value
\ell>0
of the counter
b
, let
k=\numbits(\ell)
and for
x\in[k]
, let
z_{x}=\lsb(\ell,k-x+1)
, so that
z_{1}>\ldots>z_{k}
are the nonzero bits of
\ell
.
Let
u=\sum_{x=1}^{k-1}2^{z_{x}-1}\cdot z_{x}
be simply the decimal representation of the binary number resulting from changing the least significant bit of
\ell
to zero.
Moreover, if
X_{u}
is the output at time
t^{(u)}_{i}
, then we have
X_{u}\geq\left(1+\frac{u\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{i}
, since the counter
b
is first set to
u
at time
t^{(u)}_{i}
.
Note that
Z_{i,z_{x}}
has not been changed from time
t^{(u)}_{i}
to
t^{(\ell)}_{i}
for all
x\in[k-1]
.
Since
X_{\ell}=\sum_{x=1}^{k}Z_{i,z_{x}}
, then we have

X_{\ell}=Z_{i,z_{k}}+X_{u}\geq Z_{i,z_{k}}+\left(1+\frac{u\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{i}.

Suppose by way of contradiction that
F(1,u_{i,z_{k}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{k}})>\frac{1}{2^{\beta-z_{k}-3}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
.
Recall that
Z_{i,z_{k}}
is an additive
2\eta_{z_{k}}F(1,t_{i})
approximation to
F(1,u_{i,z_{k}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{k}})
.
Hence we have

Z_{i,z_{k}}>\frac{1}{2^{\beta-z_{k}-3}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})-2\eta_{z_{k}}F(1,t_{i})>\frac{2^{z_{k}}\cdot\varepsilon}{3}\cdot F(1,t_{i}),

since
\beta={\left\lceil\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}
and
\eta_{z_{k}}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-z_{k}}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}
for
\varphi>1
.
Then we have

X_{\ell}=Z_{i,z_{k}}+X_{u}>\frac{2^{z_{k}}\cdot\varepsilon}{3}\cdot F(1,t_{i})+\left(1+\frac{u\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{i}>\left(1+\frac{\ell\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{i},

for sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
, since
2^{z_{k}}\geq(u-\ell)
and
F(1,t_{i})\geq(1-\varepsilon)Z_{i}
conditioned on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
.
But this contradicts the fact that the counter
b
is set to
\ell
at time
t^{(\ell)}_{i}
, since

X_{\ell}>\left(1+\frac{\ell\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{i}.

Thus we have
F(1,u_{i,z_{k}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{k}})\leq\frac{1}{2^{\beta-z_{k}-3}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
, which completes the inductive step.
The result then follows from observing that for
\beta={\left\lceil\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}
, we have that
\frac{1}{2^{\beta-j-3}}F(1,t_{i})\leq\frac{2^{j}\varepsilon}{64}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4

(Correctness on non-adaptive streams)
.

With probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
,
Algorithm
1
outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F
at all times on a non-adaptive stream.

Proof.

As before, let
t_{i}
be the update time during which the counter
a
in
Algorithm
1
is first set to
i
for any integer
i>0
.
For each integer
j\geq 0
, let
u_{i,j}
be the last round for which
Z_{i,j}
in
Algorithm
1
is defined.
Let
t_{i,\ell}
be the first round such that the counter
b
in
Algorithm
1
is first set to
\ell
, and we use the convention that
t_{i,0}=t_{i}
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that
Algorithm
1
outputs a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)
-approximation to the value of
F
at all times
t_{i}
, so that by
Lemma
2.2
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
.
For the remainder of the proof, we fix the integer
i
and show correctness between
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
.
Note that conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have correctness at times
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
.
By the monotonicity of
F
, it suffices to show correctness at all times
t_{i,\ell}
for each value
\ell
obtained by the counter
b
between times
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
.

Let
\ell
be a fixed value of the counter
b
,
k=\numbits(\ell)
, and
z_{x}=\lsb(\ell,x)
for
x\in[k]
so that
z_{1}>\ldots>z_{k}
are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of
\ell
.
By
Lemma
2.3
, we have for all
x\in[k]

F(1,u_{i,z_{x}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{x}})\leq\frac{1}{2^{\beta-x-3}}F(1,t_{i}),

with probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
.
Since
u_{i,z_{x}}
is the last round for which
Z_{i,z_{x}}
in
Algorithm
1
is defined and all times
t_{i,j}
for
j\leq z_{x}
are reset at that round, then we have
u_{i,z_{x}}=t_{i,z_{x+1}}
for all
x\in[k]
.
Thus since
u_{i,z_{k}}=t_{i,\ell}
, we can decompose

F(1,t_{i,\ell})=\sum_{x=1}^{k}\left(F(1,u_{i,z_{x}})-F(1,u_{i,z_{x-1}})\right)=\sum_{x=1}^{k}\left(F(1,u_{i,z_{x}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{x}})\right).

Recall that the value
X_{\ell}
of
X
output by
EstimateF
at time
t_{i,\ell}
satisfies

X_{\ell}=Z_{i}+\sum_{x=1}^{k}Z_{i,z_{x}}.

By
Lemma
2.2
,
Z_{i}
incurs at most
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)
multiplicative error to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
since
F(1,t_{i,\ell})\leq 4F(t_{i})
.
Moreover,
Z_{i,z_{k}}
is an additive
2\eta_{z_{k}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})
approximation to
F(1,u_{i,z_{k}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{k}})
.
Hence, the total additive error of
X_{\ell}
to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
is at most

\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell})+\sum_{x=1}^{k}2\eta_{z_{x}}\cdot F(1,t_{i}).

Now if the
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator uses space dependency
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}
, then
\eta_{x}=\frac{\eta}{\beta}
for all
x\in[\beta]
, so that

\sum_{x=1}^{k}2\eta_{z_{x}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})\leq\sum_{x\in[\beta]}\eta_{x}\cdot F(1,t_{i})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\cdot F(1,t_{i})

and the error is at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell})
.
On the other hand, if the
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator uses space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for
C>1
, then
\eta_{x}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-x}}
for
\varphi=2^{(C-1)/4}
implies that the error is at most

\sum_{x=1}^{k}2\eta_{z_{x}}\cdot F(1,t_{i})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell}).

Therefore,
X_{\ell}
is a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)
-approximation to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
.

Note that the final output at time
t_{i,\ell}
by
Algorithm
1
further incurs additive error at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\cdot Z_{i}
due to the rounding
\left(1+\frac{b\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{a}
in the last step.
By
Lemma
2.2
,
Z_{i}\leq\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell})
.
Hence for sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
, we have that
Algorithm
1
outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
, with probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
.
∎

Theorem 2.5

(Framework for adversarially robust algorithms on insertion-only streams)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
and
F
be a monotonic function with
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number
\lambda=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
on a stream of length
m
, with
\log m=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
.
Suppose there exists a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator for
F
that uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)+S_{2}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)\right)
bits of space for some constant
C\geq 1
and a strong tracker for
F
that use
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)+S_{2}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)\right)
bits of space and functions
S_{1},S_{2}
that depend on
F
.
Then there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for
F
that succeeds with constant probability.
For
C>1
, the algorithm uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\right)

bits of space, where
\delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\,\log n\right)}\right)
.
For
C=1
, the algorithm uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\right)

bits of space.

Proof.

Consider
Algorithm
1
.

Lemma
2.4
proves correctness over non-adaptive streams.
We now argue that
Lemma
2.4
holds over adversarial streams.
The claim follows from a similar argument to the switch-a-sketch technique of
[
BJWY20
]
.
Observe that each time either counter
a
or
b
increases, the subroutine
EstimateF
causes
Algorithm
1
to use new subroutines
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
that have not previously been revealed to the adversary.
Thus the adversarial input is independent of the randomness of
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.
Hence by the correctness guarantee over non-adaptive streams,
Algorithm
1
outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation at all times.

We now analyze the space complexity of
Algorithm
1
for a difference estimator with space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
with
C>1
.
Recall that
EstimateF
uses instance
j={\left\lfloor\frac{b+1}{2^{k}}\right\rfloor}
for
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
for each ratio parameter
\gamma_{k}=2^{k-1}\eta
and accuracy parameter
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-k}}
, where
k\in[\beta]
for
\beta=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
,
\varphi=2^{(C-1)/4}
, and
\eta=\frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}
.

By assumption, a single instance of
{\mathcal{B}}
uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)+S_{2}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)\right)

bits of space.
Since we require correctness of
\poly\left(\log n,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
instances of
{\mathcal{B}}
, it suffices to set
\delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)}\right)
.
For granularity
k
, we have
j={\left\lfloor\frac{b+1}{2^{k}}\right\rfloor}
for
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
so that there are at most
\frac{C_{2}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}
instances of
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
, for some absolute constant
C_{2}>0
.
Thus for fixed
a
,
\beta\leq\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}+1
,
\gamma_{k}=2^{k-1}\eta
, and accuracy parameter
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-k}}
, the total space for each
{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}
across the
\beta
granularities is

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{3}\gamma_{k}^{C}}{\eta_{k}^{2}2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{3}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{3}\gamma_{\beta-k}^{C}}{\eta_{\beta-k}^{2}2^{\beta-k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{3}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{4}(2^{\beta-k}\varepsilon)^{C}\varphi^{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{\beta-k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{4}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{5}2^{-Ck}\varphi^{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{-k}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{4}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon),

for some absolute constants
C_{3},C_{4},C_{5}>0
.
Since
\varphi=2^{(C-1)/4}
for
C>1
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{3}\gamma_{k}^{C}}{\eta_{k}^{2}2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{3}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{5}2^{k-Ck}2^{(Ck-k)/2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{4}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right).

Observe that for a stream with
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number
\lambda=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
, we have
a=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
, since the value of
F
increases by a constant factor each time
a
increases.
Hence, accounting for
a=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
, then
Algorithm
1
uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right)

bits of space in total for
C>1
across each of the strong trackers and difference estimators.

Finally, we observe that because we run
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\right)
instances of the subroutines, it takes
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\right)
total additional bits of space to maintain the splitting times for each of the instances over the course of a stream of length
m
, with
\log m=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
, so that the total space is

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\right).

We now analyze the space complexity for
Algorithm
1
for a difference estimator with space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
with
C=1
.
Instead of the accuracy parameter
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-k}}
for
C>1
, we now have
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta}{\beta}
for
C=1

Thus for fixed
a
,
\beta\leq\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}+1
, and
\gamma_{k}=2^{k-1}\eta
, there exist constants
C_{6},C_{7}>0
such that the total space for each
{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}
across the
\beta
granularities is

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{3}\gamma_{k}^{C}}{\eta_{k}^{2}2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{3}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{6}(2^{k-1}\varepsilon)}{\eta^{2}\beta^{-2}\cdot 2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{6}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{7}\beta^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{C_{7}}{2^{k}\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right).

Again accounting for
a=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
and the space it takes to maintain the splitting times, then
Algorithm
1
uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\right).

bits of space in total for
C>1
across each of the strong trackers and difference estimators.
∎

3
Robust
F_{2}
Estimation

In this section, we use the previous framework of
Section
2
to give an adversarially robust streaming algorithm for
F_{2}
moment estimation.
Recall that to apply
Theorem
2.5
, we require an
F_{2}
strong tracker and an
F_{2}
difference estimator.
We present these subroutines in this section.
We further optimize our algorithm beyond the guarantees of
Theorem
2.5
specifically for
F_{p}
moments, so that our final space guarantees in
Theorem
3.8
matches the best known
F_{2}
algorithm on insertion-only streams, up to lower order
\polylog\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
terms.
Finally, we show that our algorithm naturally extends to the problem of finding the
L_{2}
-heavy hitters, along with producing an estimate for the frequency of each heavy-hitter up to an additive
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)\cdot L_{2}
error.

We first recall the following
F_{2}
strong tracker.

Theorem 3.1

(Oblivious
F_{2}
strong tracking)
.

[
BDN17
]

Given an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon>0
and a failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
, let
d=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\log n\right)\right)
.
There exists an insertion-only streaming algorithm
Estimator
that uses
\mathcal{O}\left(d\log n\right)
space to provide
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-strong
F_{2}
tracking of an underlying frequency vector
f
.

To define our difference estimator, we first note that “good”
F_{2}
approximation to two vectors
u
and
v
also gives a “good” approximation to their inner product
\langle u,v\rangle
.

Lemma 3.2

(AMS
F_{2}
approximation gives inner product approximation)
.

Let vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
be a sketching matrix so that each entry
M_{i,j}
of
M
is a four-wise independent random sign scaled by
\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}
for
d=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
.
Then

\Pr{|\langle u,v\rangle-\langle Mu,Mv\rangle|\leq\varepsilon\|u\|_{2}\|v\|_{2}}\geq\frac{2}{3}.

Proof.

The proof is a standard variance reduction argument that follows immediately from
Lemma
3.3
and
Lemma
3.4
and Chebyshev’s inequality, since
M
can be viewed as taking the arithmetic mean of
d
scaled sign vectors
s_{1},\ldots,s_{d}
.
∎

Lemma 3.3

(Strong tracking of AMS inner product approximation)
.

Given vectors
0^{n}\preceq u_{1}\preceq u_{2}\preceq\ldots\preceq u_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose entries are bounded by a polynomial in
n
, there exists an algorithm that uses a sketching matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
with
d=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\log n\right)\right)
such that for
m=\poly(n)
and a fixed
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
v\succeq 0^{n}
,

|\langle u_{i},v\rangle-\langle Mu_{i},Mv\rangle|\leq\varepsilon\|u_{i}\|_{2}\|v\|_{2},

simultaneously for all
i\in[m]
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Consider a sequence of indices
t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{q}
such that
t_{i+1}
is the minimal index such that
\langle u_{t_{i+1}},v\rangle\geq\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)\langle u_{t_{i}},v\rangle
and observe that
q=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
for
m=\poly(n)
since each vector
u_{i}
has polynomially bounded entries.
Since
0^{n}\preceq u_{1}\preceq u_{2}\preceq\ldots\preceq u_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and thus both
\|u_{1}\|_{2}\leq\|u_{2}\|_{2}\leq\ldots\|u_{m}\|_{2}
and
\langle u_{1},v\rangle\leq\langle u_{2},v\rangle\leq\ldots\leq\langle u_{m},v\rangle
, it suffices to show that

|\langle u_{t_{i}},v\rangle-\langle Mu_{t_{i}},Mv\rangle|\leq\varepsilon\|u_{t_{i}}\|_{2}\|v\|_{2},

for all
i\in[q]
.
By
Lemma
3.4
and
Lemma
3.5
, we have that for any

\Pr{|\langle u_{t_{i}},v\rangle-\langle Mu_{t_{i}},Mv\rangle|\leq\varepsilon\|u_{t_{i}}\|_{2}\|v\|_{2}}\geq\frac{2}{3}

for a sketching matrix with
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
rows.
Thus by taking a sketching matrix with
d
rows, for
d=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\log n\right)\right)
, and using a standard median-of-means approach, we can take a union bound over all
i\in[q]
.
Therefore,

|\langle u_{i},v\rangle-\langle Mu_{i},Mv\rangle|\leq\varepsilon\|u_{i}\|_{2}\|v\|_{2},

simultaneously for all
i\in[m]
with probability at least
1-\delta
.
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sign vector
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
with four-wise independent entries.
For any
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right]=\langle u,v\rangle.

Proof.

By linearity of expectation, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}u_{i}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}v_{i}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}s_{i}u_{i}s_{j}v_{j}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}u_{i}s_{j}v_{j}\right].

Since the variables
s_{i}
are
4
-wise independent sign variables
\{-1,+1\}
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}s_{j}\right]=1
for
i=j
and
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}s_{j}\right]=0
for
i\neq j
.
Hence,

\mathbb{E}\left[\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}v_{i}=\langle u,v\rangle.

∎

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sign vector
\{-1,+1\}^{n}
with four-wise independent entries.
For any
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

\Var\left(\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right)\leq(\langle u,v\rangle)^{2}.

Proof.

Since
\Var{\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right)^{2}\right]
, it follows by linearity of expectation that

\displaystyle\Var\left(\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}u_{i}\right)^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}v_{i}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}s_{i}s_{j}s_{k}s_{\ell}u_{i}v_{j}u_{k}v_{\ell}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}s_{j}s_{k}s_{\ell}u_{i}v_{j}u_{k}v_{\ell}\right].

Since the variables
s_{i}
are
4
-wise independent sign variables
\{-1,+1\}
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}s_{j}s_{k}s_{\ell}\right]=1
if
i,j,k,\ell
consists of two (possibly not distinct) pairs of indices, e.g.,
i=j
and
k=\ell
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[s_{i}s_{j}s_{k}s_{\ell}\right]=0
otherwise.
Therefore,

\Var\left(\langle s,u\rangle\cdot\langle s,v\rangle\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{i}v_{i}u_{j}v_{j}=\left(\langle u,v\rangle\right)^{2}.

∎

We now give our
F_{2}
difference estimator using the inner product approximation property.

Lemma 3.6

(
F_{2}
difference estimator)
.

There exists a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-
difference estimator
for
F_{2}
that uses space

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min(1,\varepsilon)}{32}
and
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{2}
.
Given an oblivious stream
{\mathcal{S}}
, let
v
be the frequency vector induced by the updates of
{\mathcal{S}}
from time
t_{1}
to
t_{2}
and
w_{t}
be the frequency vector induced by updates from time
t_{2}
to
t
exclusive, with
\gamma\cdot F_{2}(v)\geq F_{2}(w_{t})
.
Let
M(x)
represent the output of the algorithm
Estimator
with accuracy parameter
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and failure rate
\delta^{\prime}
on an input vector
x
induced by a stream.
Hence we have
M(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
d=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)
.
We remark that the
\log\log n
term in the dimension of
d
from
Theorem
3.1
results from the analysis of
[
BDN17
]
breaking the stream into
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
times when the value of
F_{2}
on the stream doubles.
We do not need this
\log\log n
term since
\gamma\cdot F_{2}(v)\geq F_{2}(w_{t})
for an absolute constant
\gamma>0
implies that the value of the difference can only double a constant number of times.

By
Lemma
3.2
, we have that with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
,

|\langle v,w_{t}\rangle-\langle M(v),M(w_{t})\rangle|\leq\frac{4\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\|v\|_{2}\|w_{t}\|_{2}.

Recalling the identity

F_{2}(v+w_{t})-F_{2}(v)=2\langle v,w_{t}\rangle+\|w_{t}\|_{2}^{2},

we thus have that

\displaystyle\big|\left[F_{2}(v+w_{t})-F_{2}(v)\right]-

\displaystyle\left[2\langle M(v),M(w_{t})\rangle+\|M(w_{t})\|_{2}^{2}\right]\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\left[2\langle v,w_{t}\rangle-2\langle M(v),M(w_{t})\rangle\right]+\left[\|w_{t}\|_{2}^{2}-\|M(w_{t})\|_{2}^{2}\right]\big|

\displaystyle\leq 8\varepsilon^{\prime}\|v\|_{2}\|w_{t}\|_{2}+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\|w_{t}\|_{2}^{2},

since
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min(1,\varepsilon)}{32}
.
Because
\gamma\cdot F(v)\geq F(w_{t})
, then we have
\|w_{t}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\gamma}\|v\|_{2}
, so that the total error of the estimator is at most

\frac{8\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\|v\|_{2}\|w_{t}\|_{2}+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\|w_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 8\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot F(v)+3\varepsilon^{\prime}\gamma\cdot F(v).

Hence for
\gamma\leq 2
, the error is at most
14\varepsilon^{\prime}\cdot F(v)\leq\varepsilon\cdot F(v)
.
Since
Theorem
3.1
and
Lemma
3.2
each hold with probability at least
1-\delta^{\prime}
for
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{2}
, then the total probability of success is at least
1-\delta
.

By
Theorem
3.1
, each of the two instances of
Estimator
uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma\log n}{(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}+\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}+\log\log n\right)\right)

bits of space, for
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\min(1,\varepsilon)}{32}
and
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{2}
.
However, the
\log\log n
term in the space requirement of
Estimator
results from the need to union bound over
\log n
locations where the value of
F_{2}
on the stream increases by a factor of
2
.
Since
\gamma
is upper bounded by a constant, we only need a union bound over a constant number of locations.
Hence, the space complexity of the
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator for
F_{2}
is

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right).

∎

Theorem 3.7
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for
F_{2}
that uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\log n\right)\right)
bits of space and succeeds with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
.

Proof.

F_{2}
is a monotonic function with
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number
\lambda=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\right)
, by
Observation
1.12
.
By
Lemma
3.6
and
Theorem
3.1
, there exists a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator that uses space

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)

and there exists an oblivious strong tracker for
F_{2}
that uses space

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\log n\right)\right).

Therefore by using the
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator and the oblivious strong tracker for
F_{2}
in the framework of
Algorithm
1
, then
Theorem
2.5
with
S_{1}=\log n\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\log n\right)
,
C=1
, and
S_{2}=0
proves that there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for
F_{2}
that uses space
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{2}n\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\log n\right)\right)
and succeeds with constant probability.
∎

Optimized
F_{2}
Algorithm.

Instead of maintaining all sketches
{\mathcal{B}}_{a}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{a,j}
simultaneously, it suffices to maintain sketches
{\mathcal{B}}_{a}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{a,j}
for
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
values of
a
at a time.
Namely, the counter
a
in
Algorithm
1
tracks the active instances
{\mathcal{B}}_{a}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{a,j}
output by the algorithm.
Because the output increases by a factor of
2
each time
a
increases, it suffices to maintain the sketches
{\mathcal{B}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{i,c}
for only the smallest
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
values of
i
that are at least
a
.
Any larger index will have only missed
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\right)
fraction of the
F_{2}
of the stream and thus still output a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation.

Theorem 3.8

(Adversarially robust
F_{2}
streaming algorithm)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for
F_{2}
that succeeds with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
and uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\log^{4}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\log n\right)\right)
bits of space.

Proof.

At any point, there are
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
active indices
a
and
\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
active indices
c
corresponding to sketches
{\mathcal{B}}_{a}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{a,c}
.
Recall from
Theorem
2.5
that for fixed
a
, the total space for each
{\mathcal{A}}_{a,j}
across the
\beta
granularities is

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{2}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right),

where we have
S_{1}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)=\log n\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}+\log\log n\right)
and
S_{2}=0
for our
F_{2}
difference estimator and
F_{2}
strong tracker.

Whereas
Theorem
2.5
bounds the counter
a
by
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
so that we maintain
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
simultaneous instances of
i
for the algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}_{i,j}
, we observed that it suffices to maintain at most
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
simultaneous active values of
i
.
Since there are still
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
total indices
a
over the course of the stream, then each sketch must have failure probability
\frac{\delta}{\poly\left(\log n,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}
for the entire algorithm to have failure probability
\delta=\frac{2}{3}
.
Finally, there are
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
active indices of
a
, consisting of
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
subroutines.
Thus, it takes
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
additional bits of space to store the splitting times for each of the
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
active subroutines across a stream of length
m
, with
\log m=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
.
Thus the total space required is
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n\log^{4}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+\log\log n\right)\right)
.
∎

Heavy-hitters.

As a simple corollary, note that our framework also solves the
L_{2}
-heavy hitters problem.
By running separate
L_{2}
-heavy hitters algorithms corresponding to each difference estimator
{\mathcal{A}}
and strong tracker
{\mathcal{B}}
, with the heavy-hitter threshold corresponding to the accuracy of each procedure, we obtain a list containing all the possible heavy-hitters along with an estimated frequency of each item in the list.

Theorem 3.9
.

[
BCI+17
]

For any
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,1)
, there exists a streaming algorithm
(\varepsilon,\delta)-{\textsc{BPTree}}
, that with probability at least
1-\delta
, returns a set of
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
-heavy hitters containing every
\varepsilon
-heavy hitter and an approximate frequency for every item returned with additive error
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot L_{2}
.
The algorithm uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\log\frac{1}{\delta\varepsilon}\right)(\log n+\log m)\right)
bits of space.

Theorem 3.10

(Adversarially robust
L_{2}
-heavy hitters streaming algorithm)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm
HeavyHitters
that solves the
L_{2}
