\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
active subroutines across a stream of length
m
, with
\log m=\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
.
Therefore, the total space required is

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log^{4}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\log\log n+\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\log n\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right).

∎

7
Framework for Streams with Bounded Twist Number

Recall that the previous framework of
Theorem
2.5
in
Section
2
was only suited for insertion-only streams.
In this section, we modify the framework of
Algorithm
1
to handle
F_{p}
moment estimation on turnstile streams.

The main difference for turnstile streams is that the value of
F
on the underlying frequency vector induced by the stream can decrease as well as increase.
Thus if we assign an algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
to be revealed when the value of
F
exceeds
2^{j}
, this may happen multiple times.
However, once
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
is revealed for the first time, then the input to the algorithm is no longer independent to the randomness of the algorithm, due to the adversary.
Hence, we can no longer use
{\mathcal{A}}_{j}
to estimate the value of
F
if it decreases and then exceeds
2^{j}
again.

Nevertheless, note that each time the value of
F
increases or decreases by a factor of roughly
(1+\varepsilon)
, the value of the
(\varepsilon,m)
-twist number
\lambda
increases.
Therefore,
\lambda
governs the number of times the value of
F
can exceed
2^{j}
, going from
2^{j-1}
.
Similarly,
\lambda
governs the number of times the value of
F
can exceed
(1+\varepsilon)^{Ci}2^{j}
, going from
(1+\varepsilon)^{C(i-1)}2^{j}
for any constant
C
.
Thus if
\lambda
is given in advance, the algorithm can maintain the appropriate number of independent instances of the difference estimators
{\mathcal{B}}
and the streaming algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}
to handle each time the value of
F
crosses a threshold.
We give the algorithm in full in
Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

Framework for Robust Algorithms on Turnstile Streams

1:

Stream
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in[n]
of updates to coordinates of an underlying frequency vector, accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, difference estimator
{\mathcal{B}}
for
F
, oblivious strong tracker
{\mathcal{A}}
for
F
,
\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{32},m\right)
-twist number
\lambda

2:

Robust
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F

3:

\delta\leftarrow\frac{1}{\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)}
,
\zeta\leftarrow\frac{2}{2^{(C-1)/4}-1}
,
\eta\leftarrow\frac{\varepsilon}{64\zeta}
,
\beta\leftarrow{\left\lceil\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}
,
\tau\leftarrow 0

4:

a\leftarrow 0
,
\varphi\leftarrow 2^{(C-1)/4}
,
\gamma_{j}\leftarrow 2^{j-1}\eta

5:

For
j\in[\beta]
,
\eta_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\eta}{\beta}
if
C=1
,
\eta_{j}\leftarrow\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-j}}
if
C>1
.

\triangleright
Accuracy for each difference estimator

6:

t_{i,j}\leftarrow\infty
for
i\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\lambda\right)
,
j\in[\beta]

\triangleright
Counters/Timestamps

7:

for
each update
u_{t}\in[n]
,
t\in[m]

do

8:

j\leftarrow{\left\lceil\eta\tau\right\rceil}

9:

X\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{a+1,j}(1,t,\eta,\delta)

10:

if

X>2^{a}

then

\triangleright
Switch sketch at top layer

11:

a\leftarrow a+1
,
b\leftarrow 0
,
Z_{a}\leftarrow X
,
t_{a,j}\leftarrow\min(t,t_{a,j})
for
j\in[\beta]
.

12:

if

X<2^{a-1}

then

\triangleright
Switch sketch at top layer

13:

a\leftarrow a-1
,
b\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\cdot(X-Z_{a})\right\rfloor}

14:

X\leftarrow{\textsc{EstimateF}}

\triangleright
Compute estimator
X
for
F
using unrevealed sketch

15:

if

X>\left(1+\frac{(b+1)\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{a}

then

\triangleright
Switch sketch at lower layer

16:

\tau\leftarrow\tau+1
,
b\leftarrow b+1
,
k\leftarrow\lsb(b,1)
,
j\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{\tau}{2^{k}}\right\rfloor}

17:

Z_{a,k}\leftarrow{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\eta_{k},\delta)

\triangleright
Freeze old sketch

18:

t_{a,j}\leftarrow t
for
j\in[k]
.

\triangleright
Update difference estimator times

19:

else

if

X<\left(1+\frac{(b-1)\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{a}

then

\triangleright
Switch sketch at lower layer

20:

\tau\leftarrow\tau+1
,
b\leftarrow b-1
,
k\leftarrow\lsb(b,1)
,
j\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{\tau}{2^{k}}\right\rfloor}

21:

Z_{a,k}\leftarrow{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta)

\triangleright
Freeze old sketch

22:

t_{a,j}\leftarrow t
for
j\in[k]
.

\triangleright
Update difference estimator times

23:

else

if
if there exist
j,k
with unfrozen
{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta)>\frac{\gamma_{j}}{2}X

then

24:

\tau\leftarrow\tau+1
,
j\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{\tau}{2^{k}}\right\rfloor}

25:

Z_{a,k}\leftarrow Z_{a,k}+{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}(1,t_{a,k},t,\gamma_{k},\eta_{k},\delta)

\triangleright
Freeze old sketch

26:

t_{a,j}\leftarrow t
for
j\in[k]
.

27:

return

\left(1+\frac{b\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{a}

\triangleright
Output estimate for round
t

Algorithm 4

Subroutine
EstimateF
, modified for
Algorithm
3

1:

X\leftarrow Z_{a}
,
k\leftarrow\numbits(b+1)
,
z_{i}\leftarrow\lsb(b+1,k+1-i)
for
i\in[k]
.

2:

\triangleright

z_{1}>\ldots>z_{k}
are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of
b+1
.

3:

for

1\leq j\leq k-1

do

\triangleright
Compile previous frozen components for estimator
X

4:

X\leftarrow X+Z_{a,j}

5:

j\leftarrow{\left\lfloor\frac{\tau}{2^{z_{k}}}\right\rfloor}

6:

X\leftarrow X+{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}\left(1,t^{(j)}_{a,z_{k}},t,\gamma_{z_{k}},\eta_{z_{k}},\delta\right)

\triangleright
Use unrevealed sketch for last component

7:

return

X

The proof of correctness on non-adaptive streams follows similarly from the same argument as
Lemma
2.4
.
The main difference is that because the value of
F
on the stream can both increase and decrease, we must be a little more careful in defining the times for which the difference estimators reveal their outputs.
In addition, to ensure the correctness of the difference estimator, we must facilitate the conditions required for the difference estimator.
Thus we restart a difference estimator each time the value of the suffix has become sufficiently large even if the difference itself has not become large.
This is a subtle issue for which we use the definition of twist number, rather than the simpler definition of flip number.

Lemma 7.1

(Correctness on non-adaptive streams)
.

With probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
,
Algorithm
3
outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F
at all times on a non-adaptive stream.

Proof.

Let
t^{(y)}_{i}
be the time in the stream at which the counter
a
in
Algorithm
3
is set to
i
for the
y
-th time, for integers
i,y>0
.
For each integer
j\geq 0
, let
u_{i,j}
be the last round for which
Z_{i,j}
in
Algorithm
3
is defined.
Let
t_{i,\ell}
be the last round such that the counter
b
in
Algorithm
3
is first set to
\ell
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that
Algorithm
3
outputs a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{32}\right)
-approximation to the value of
F
at all times
t^{(y)}_{i}
, so that by
Lemma
2.2
,
\mathcal{E}_{1}
holds with probability at least
1-\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda\delta\log n}{\varepsilon}\right)
, since there are at most
\lambda
times
\{t^{(y)}_{i}\}
by the definition of the twist number.
For the remainder of the proof, we fix the integers
i
and
y
and show correctness between
t^{(y)}_{i}
and the first time
t>t^{(y)}_{i}
such that either
t=t^{(y^{\prime})}_{i+1}
or
t=t^{(y^{\prime})}_{i-1}
for some
y^{\prime}
.
Without loss of generality, suppose
t=t^{(y^{\prime})}_{i+1}
.
We then show correctness at all times
t^{(\ell,r)}_{i}
for each value
\ell
obtained by the counter
b
between times
t^{(y)}_{i}
and
t^{(y^{\prime})}_{i+1}
.
The remainder of the argument proceeds similarly to
Lemma
2.4
.

Let
\ell
be a fixed value of the counter
b
,
k=\numbits(\ell)
, and
z_{x}=\lsb(\ell,x)
for
x\in[k]
so that
z_{1}>\ldots>z_{k}
are the nonzero bits in the binary representation of
\ell
.
By similar reasoning to
Lemma
2.3
, we have for all
x\in[k]

F(1,u_{i,z_{x}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{x}})\leq\frac{1}{2^{\beta-x-3}}F(1,t_{i}),

with probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
.
Since
u_{i,z_{x}}
is the last round for which
Z_{i,z_{x}}
in
Algorithm
3
is defined and all times
t_{i,j}
for
j\leq z_{x}
are reset at that round, then we have
u_{i,z_{x}}=t_{i,z_{x+1}}
for all
x\in[k]
.
Thus for
u_{i,z_{k}}=t_{i,\ell}
, we can decompose

F(1,t_{i,\ell})=\sum_{x=1}^{k}\left(F(1,u_{i,z_{x}})-F(1,u_{i,z_{x-1}})\right)=\sum_{x=1}^{k}\left(F(1,u_{i,z_{x}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{x}})\right).

Recall that the value
X_{\ell}
of
X
output by
EstimateF
at time
t_{i,\ell}
satisfies
X_{\ell}=Z_{i}+\sum_{x=1}^{k}Z_{i,z_{x}}
.
By
Lemma
2.2
,
Z_{i}
incurs at most
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)
multiplicative error to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
since
F(1,t_{i,\ell})\leq 4F(t_{i})
.
Moreover,
Z_{i,z_{k}}
is an additive
2\eta_{z_{k}}F(1,t_{i})
approximation to
F(1,u_{i,z_{k}})-F(1,t_{i,z_{k}})
.
Hence, the total additive error of
X_{\ell}
to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
is at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell})+\sum_{x=1}^{k}2\eta_{z_{k}}F(1,t_{i})
.
Now if the
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator has space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
with
C=1
, then
\eta_{z_{k}}=\frac{\eta}{\beta}
.
Otherwise if
C>1
, then
\eta_{z_{k}}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-z_{k}}}
, with
\varphi>1
.
In both cases, we have that the total additive error is at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell})
, since
\beta={\left\lceil\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil}
.
Therefore,
X_{\ell}
is a
\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)
-approximation to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
.

Note that the final output at time
t_{i,\ell}
by
Algorithm
3
further incurs additive error at most
\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\cdot Z_{i}
due to the rounding
\left(1+\frac{b\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot Z_{a}
in the last step.
By
Lemma
2.2
,
Z_{i}\leq\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\right)\cdot F(1,t_{i,\ell})
.
Hence for sufficiently small constant
\varepsilon>0
, we have that
Algorithm
3
outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F(1,t_{i,\ell})
, with probability at least
1-\delta\cdot\poly\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log n\right)
.
∎

Theorem 7.2

(Framework for adversarially robust algorithms on turnstile streams)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta>0
and
F
be a monotonic function with
(\eta,m)
-twist number
\lambda
, where
\eta=\frac{\varepsilon}{32}
.
Suppose there exists a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator with space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
for
C\geq 1
and a strong tracker for
F
on dynamic streams that use
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}S_{F}(n,\delta,\varepsilon)\right)
bits of space.
Then there exists an adversarially robust dynamic streaming algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
approximation for
F
that succeeds with constant probability.
For
C>1
, the algorithm uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right)
bits of space, where
\delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\poly\left(\lambda,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right)
.
For
C=1
, the algorithm uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right)
bits of space.

Proof.

Consider
Algorithm
3
and note that
Lemma
7.1
proves correctness over non-adaptive streams.
We now argue that
Lemma
7.1
holds with over adversarial streams, using the same argument as
Theorem
2.5
and the switch-a-sketch proof by
[
BJWY20
]
.
Observe that each time either counter
a
or
b
either increases or decreases, the modified subroutine
EstimateF
compels
Algorithm
3
to use new subroutines
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
that have not previously been revealed to the adversary.
In particular, since
EstimateF
sets
j={\left\lfloor\frac{b+1}{2^{z_{k}}}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lceil\frac{\tau}{2^{k}}\right\rceil}
, then since the
\eta
twist number has only reached
\tau
, the value of
j
ensures that
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
has not been previously revealed.
Hence, the input is independent of the internal randomness of
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
until the counter
b
reaches
b=j
for the
{\left\lceil\frac{\tau}{2^{k}}\right\rceil}
-th time, at which point
{\mathcal{B}}_{i,j}
is revealed once and not used again.

It remains to analyze the space complexity of
Algorithm
3
.
We first analyze the space complexity for a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator with space dependency
\frac{\gamma^{C}}{\varepsilon^{2}}
and
C>1
.
Observe that
a=\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\lambda\right)
and across the
\beta
granularities, each value of
\eta_{k}
is invoked at most
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda}{2^{k}}\right)
times.
Since we require correctness of
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\lambda\right)
instances of
{\mathcal{B}}
, it suffices to set
\delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\poly\left(\lambda,\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)}\right)
.
By assumption, a single instance of
{\mathcal{B}}
with granularity
\eta_{k}
uses at most
\frac{C_{1}}{(\eta_{k})^{2}}S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)
bits of space, for some constant
C_{1}>0
.
Thus for fixed
a
,
\beta\leq\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}+1
,
\gamma_{k}=2^{k-1}\eta
, and accuracy parameter
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta}{\varphi^{\beta-k}}
, the total space for each
{\mathcal{B}}_{a,j}
across the
\beta
granularities and the values of the counter
a
is

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{1}\lambda\gamma_{k}^{C}}{\eta_{k}^{2}2^{k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{1}\lambda\gamma_{\beta-k}^{C}}{\eta_{\beta-k}^{2}2^{\beta-k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{2}\lambda(2^{\beta-k}\varepsilon)^{C}\varphi^{2k}}{\varepsilon^{2}\cdot 2^{\beta-k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{3}\lambda 2^{-Ck}\varphi^{2k}}{\varepsilon\cdot 2^{-k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon),

for some absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
.
Since
\varphi=2^{(C-1)/4}
for
C>1
, then there exists a constant
C_{4}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{1}\lambda\gamma_{k}^{C}}{\eta_{k}^{2}2^{k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{4}\lambda 2^{k-Ck}2^{(Ck-k)/2}}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right).

Moreover, since each strong tracker
{\mathcal{A}}
is used when the value of
F
increases by a constant factor, then
\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\lambda\right)
instances of
{\mathcal{A}}
are required.
Hence, the total space required for
C>1
is
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right)
.

For
C=1
, we recall that
\eta_{k}=\frac{\eta}{\beta}
, with
\beta\leq\log\frac{8}{\varepsilon}+1
.
Thus the total space is

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{1}\lambda\gamma_{k}^{C}}{\eta_{k}^{2}2^{k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{5}\lambda(2^{k-1}\varepsilon)}{\eta^{2}\beta^{-2}\cdot 2^{k}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\beta}\frac{C_{6}\lambda\beta^{2}\varepsilon}{\eta^{2}}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\log^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot S_{F}(n,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\right).

∎

As a direct application of
Theorem
7.2
, we obtain the following:

Theorem 7.3
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an adversarially robust streaming algorithm on turnstile streams with
(\varepsilon,m)
twist number
\lambda
that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation for
F_{p}
with
p\in[0,2]
that succeeds with probability at least
\frac{2}{3}
and uses
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\varepsilon}\log^{2}n\right)
bits of space.

8
Framework for Sliding Window Algorithms

In this section, we describe a general framework for norm estimation in the sliding window model, using the sketch stitching and granularity changing technique.
We first require the following background on sliding windows algorithms.

Definition 8.1

(Smooth function)
.

Given adjacent substreams
A
,
B
, and
C
, a function
f
is
(\alpha,\beta)
-smooth if
(1-\beta)f(A\cup B)\leq f(B)
, then
(1-\alpha)f(A\cup B\cup C)\leq f(B\cup C)
for some parameters
0<\beta\leq\alpha<1
.

Intuitively, once a suffix of a data stream becomes a
(1\pm\beta)
-approximation for a smooth function, then it is
always
a
(1\pm\alpha)
-approximation, regardless of the subsequent updates that arrive in the stream.
Smooth functions are integral to the smooth histogram framework for sliding window algorithms.

Smooth Histograms.

Braverman and Ostrovsky introduced the
smooth histogram
, an elegant framework that solves a large number of problems in the sliding window model, such as
L_{p}
norm estimation, longest increasing subsequence, geometric mean estimation, or other weakly additive functions
[
BO07
]
.
Thus the smooth histogram data structure maintains a number of timestamps throughout the data stream, along with a streaming algorithm for each timestamp that stores a sketch of all the elements seen from the timestamp.
The timestamps maintain the invariant that at most three checkpoints produce values that are within
(1-\beta)
of each other, since any two of the sketches would always output values that are within
(1-\alpha)
afterwards.
Hence if the function is polynomially bounded, then the smooth histogram data structure only needs a logarithmic number of timestamps.

We now define the following variant of a difference estimator.

Definition 8.2

(Suffix-Pivoted Difference Estimator)
.

Given a stream
{\mathcal{S}}
and fixed times
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
, and
t_{3}
, let frequency vectors
u
and
v
be induced by the updates of
{\mathcal{S}}
between times
[t_{1},t_{2})
and
[t_{2},t_{3})
.
Given an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon>0
and a failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
, a streaming algorithm
{\mathcal{C}}(t_{1},t_{2},t,\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
is a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-
suffix difference estimator
for a function
F
if, with probability at least
1-\delta
, it outputs an additive
\varepsilon\cdot F(v+w_{t})
approximation to
F(u+v+w_{t})-F(v+w_{t})
for all frequency vectors
w_{t}
induced by
[t_{3},t)
for times
t>t_{3}
, given
\min(F(u),F(u+v)-F(v))\leq\gamma\cdot F(v)
for a ratio parameter
\gamma\in(0,1]
.

Observe that the difference between
Definition
2.1
and
Definition
8.2
is that the fixed-prefix difference estimator approximates
F(v+w_{t})-F(v)
when the contribution to
F
of the first frequency vector
v
that arrives in the stream is much larger than that of
w_{t}
, while the suffix-pivoted difference estimator approximates
F(v+w_{t})-F(w_{t})
when
F(w_{t})
is larger than
F(v)
.
Nevertheless, we prove that our fixed-prefix difference estimators for
F_{p}
moment estimation with
p\in(0,2]
can also be adjusted to form suffix-pivoted difference estimators.
Unless otherwise noted, the difference estimators in this section refer to the suffix-pivoted difference estimators rather than the fixed-prefix difference estimators.

We adapt our sketch stitching and granularity changing technique to the sliding window model by focusing on the suffix of the stream, since prefixes of the stream may expire.
We thus run the highest accuracy algorithms, the separate streaming algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}
, on various suffixes of the stream similar to the smooth histogram framework.
It follows from smoothness that we maintain an instance of
{\mathcal{A}}
starting at some time
t_{0}\leq m-W+1
, whose output is within a factor
2
of the value of
F
on the sliding window.
Our task is then to remove the extraneous contribution of the updates between times
t_{0}
and
m-W+1
, i.e., the starting time of the sliding window.

We partition the substream of these updates into separate blocks based on their contribution to the value of
F
by guessing
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
values for the final value of
F
on the sliding window and forming new difference estimators at level
j
when the value of
F
on each block has exceeded a
\frac{1}{2^{j}}
fraction of the corresponding guess.
We terminate a guess when there are more than
100\cdot 2^{j}
blocks in that level, indicating that the guess is too low.
We can maintain separate sketches for these blocks, with varying granularities, and stitch these sketches together at the end.
We give our algorithm in full in
Algorithm
5
.

Interpretation of
Algorithm
5
.

We now translate between the previous intuition and the pseudocode of
Algorithm
5
.
At each time,
Algorithm
5
only runs two subroutines:
GuessAndUpdate
and
MergeSW
.
The first subroutine
GuessAndUpdate
creates new instances of each algorithm (both a streaming algorithm approximating
F
on a suffix of the stream and a difference estimator starting at each time) at each time in the stream.
Moreover,
GuessAndUpdate
partitions the stream into blocks for the difference estimator by using exponentially increasing guesses for the value of
F
at the end of the stream to assist with appropriate granularity for each block.
The second subroutine
MergeSW
performs maintenance on the data structure to ensure that there are not too many instances that have been created by the first subroutine that are simultaneously running.
Namely,
MergeSW
deletes algorithms running on suffixes of the stream that output a similar value, so that the number of remaining algorithms is logarithmic rather than linear.
Similarly,
MergeSW
merges two difference estimators when it is clear their combined contribution is still too small.
Finally at the end of the stream,
StitchSW
creates an estimate for the value of
F
on the stream by stitching together the estimates output by each difference estimator.
Although
Algorithm
1
is notationally heavy, each timestamp
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}
should be associated with (1) a guess
k\in[C\log n]
for the value of
F
at the end of the stream, (2) an index
i\in\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
roughly associated with the number of times
F
has
actually
doubled in the stream so far, (3) a granularity
j
, and (4) the number of the block
\ell
in granularity
j
.

\triangleright

Algorithm 8

Subroutine

of
Algorithm

: output estimate of
F_{p}
on the sliding window

1:

Let

be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.

2:

Let

n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)

3:

c_{0}\leftarrow t_{i}

4:

X\leftarrow{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,\eta,\delta)

5:

for

j\in[\beta]

do

\triangleright
Stitch sketches

6:

Let
a
be the smallest index such that
t^{(k)}_{i,j,a}\geq c_{j-1}

7:

Let
b
be the largest index such that
t^{(k)}_{i,j,b}\leq m-W+1

8:

c_{j}\leftarrow t^{(k)}_{i,j,b}

9:

Y_{j}\leftarrow\sum_{k=a}^{b-1}{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,k},t^{(k)}_{i,j,k+1},m,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)

10:

return

Z:=X-\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}Y_{j}

Recall that we abuse notation so that
W
represents the active updates in the stream.
Thus we use
F_{p}(W)
to denote the
F_{p}
moment of the active elements in the sliding window, i.e., if
m<W
, then
F_{p}(W)=F_{p}(1:m)
and if
m\geq W
, then
F_{p}(W)=F_{p}(1:m-W+1)
.
We first show that the timestamps
t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}
mark suffixes of the stream in which the moment roughly double, so that
F_{p}(t_{1},m)\geq\ldots\geq F_{p}(t_{s},m)
and
2^{s-i}\cdot F_{p}(t_{s},m)\geq F_{p}(t_{i},m)
for each
i\in[s]
.
Since
W
is oblivious to the algorithim until the
StitchSW
subroutine, it suffices to show that if
t_{i}\leq m-W+1\leq t_{i+1}
, then
F_{p}(W)\leq F_{p}(t_{i},m)\leq 2F_{p}(W)
and
\frac{1}{2}F_{p}(W)\leq F_{p}(t_{i+1},m)\leq F_{p}(W)
.

We first show that the top level gives a constant factor approximation to
F(W)
.
The intuition behind the lemma is that the maintenance procedure
MergeSW
only deletes an instance of an algorithm if the instance is sandwiched between two other algorithms whose values are within a factor of
(1-1/8^{q})
, where the function is
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{q})
-smooth.
Thus by the definition of smoothness, we should expect the other two algorithms to always produce values that are within a factor of two, regardless of the subsequent updates of the stream.
This is important because it allows us to translate between the values of various suffixes of the stream, e.g.,
F(t_{i},m)\leq 2F(W)
implies that additive
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(t_{i},m)
error gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
-multiplicative error to
F(W)
.

Lemma 8.3

(Constant factor partitions in top level)
.

Let
i
be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.
Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all subroutines
{\mathcal{A}}
in
Algorithm
5
succeed.
Then conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
,
F(W)\leq F(t_{i},m)\leq 2F(W)
and
\frac{1}{2}F(W)\leq F(t_{i+1},m)\leq F(W)
.

Proof.

Observe that in the
MergeSW
subroutine, we only delete a timestamp
t_{i^{\prime}}
if
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i^{\prime}+2},t,\eta,\delta)\geq(1-1/8^{q}){\mathcal{A}}(t_{i^{\prime}},t,\eta,\delta)
at some point
t
.
Since
\eta=\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{20}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{1}{1024}
, we have that conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
,
F(t_{i^{\prime}+2},t)\left(1+\frac{1}{1024}\right)\geq(1-1/8^{q})\left(1-\frac{1}{1024}\right)F(t_{i^{\prime}},t)
.
Thus,
F(t_{i^{\prime}+2},t)\geq(1-1/4^{q})F(t_{i^{\prime}},t)
.

We have either
t_{i}=m-W+1
and
t_{i+1}=m-W+2
in which case the statement is trivially true or timestamps
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
at some point
t\leq m
must have satisfied
F(t_{i+1},t)\geq(1-1/4^{q})F(t_{i},t)
so that
t_{i}+1
was removed from the set of timestamps.
Thus,
F(t_{i+1},t)\geq(1-1/4^{q})F(t_{i},t)
and recall that we assume
F
is
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{q})
-smooth.
By the definition of smoothness,
F(t_{i+1},t)\geq(1-1/4^{q})F(t_{i},t)
implies
F(t_{i+1},m)\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot F(t_{i},m)
for all
m\geq t
.
Since
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
, then
F(t_{i},m)\geq F(m-W+1,m)=F(W)\geq F(t_{i+1},m)
and the conclusion follows.
∎

We now show that our difference estimators are well-defined and thus give good approximations to the differences that they estimate.
Although this sounds trivial, we remark that a pre-condition to a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-difference estimator to succeed on the approximation of a difference
F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})
, we must have
F(v+u_{1})-F(u_{1})\leq\gamma\cdot F(v+u_{1})
, which requires proving properties about the specific partition of the stream.
On the other hand, since
GuessAndUpdate
partitions the stream into blocks for each difference estimator based on their contribution, we show that the pre-condition holds by construction.

Lemma 8.4

(Accuracy of difference estimators)
.

Let
i
be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.
Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all subroutines
{\mathcal{A}}
and
SDiffEst
in
Algorithm
5
succeed.
Then conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have that for each
j\in[\beta]
and all
\ell
,
{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1},t_{i,j,\ell},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
gives an additive
\eta\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}:t)
approximation to
F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1}:t)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}:t)
.

Proof.

Recall that for a suffix-pivoted difference estimator to be well-defined, we require
F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1}:t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell})\leq\gamma_{j}\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}:x)

at some time
x
.
Since
\gamma_{j}=2^{3-j}
and
GuessAndUpdate
creates a new partition at level
j
when
{\mathcal{A}}(t^{(k)}_{s+1,j,r_{k}},t-1,1,\delta)\leq n^{C}/2^{j+k+10}
, then it follows that
F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1}:t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell})\leq\gamma_{j}\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}:x)
.
Thus the conditions for a suffix-pivoted difference estimator hold, so
{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},m,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
gives an additive
\eta\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}:t)
approximation to
F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1}:m)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}:m)
by the guarantees of the difference estimator.
∎

We now lower bound the values of each difference.
This is important for bounding the number of difference estimators that are used to stitch together the estimate at the end of the stream.
For example, without a lower bound on the difference estimators at each level, it could be possible that we use a linear number of difference estimators to form our estimate of
F(W)
, in which case the space would be too large.
Using the following lower bound on the difference estimator, we ultimately show only a logarithmic number of difference estimators is used to form our estimate of
F(W)
.
The intuition behind the proof of
Lemma
8.5
is that if the contributions of the difference estimators of two consecutive blocks are
both
small, then
MergeSW
will end up merging the blocks.
Hence, we should expect the contribution of any two consecutive blocks to be sufficiently large.

Lemma 8.5

(Geometric lower bounds on splitting times)
.

Let
i
be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.
Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all subroutines
{\mathcal{A}}
and
SDiffEst
in
Algorithm
5
succeed.
For any
\ell\in[r_{k}-2]
, let
u
denote the frequency vector between times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}
and
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+2}
and let
w
denote the frequency vector between times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+2}
and
t
.
Finally, let
w
denote the frequency vector between times
t_{i}
and
t
.
Then conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have that

F(u+v)-F(v)\geq 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(w).

Proof.

Let
u_{1}
denote the frequency vector between times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}
and
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}
and
u_{2}
denote the frequency vector between times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}
and
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+2}
.
Recall that
MergeSW
merges two instances of
SDiffEst
when

\displaystyle{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)+{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)\leq 2^{-j-10}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{i}(t_{i},t,\eta,\delta).

Since we do not remove
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}
from the list of timestamps, then we have

{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell-1},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)+{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)>2^{-j-10}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{i}(t_{i},t,\eta,\delta).

Since the difference estimators are well-defined by
Lemma
8.4
, then
{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
is at most a
2
-approximation to
F(u_{1}+u_{2}+v)-F(u_{2}+v)
and we similarly obtain a
2
-approximation to
F(u_{2}+v)-F(v)
.
Therefore,

F(u)-F(v)=F(u_{1}+u_{2}+v)-F(v)\geq 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(w).

∎

We next bound the number of level
j
difference estimators that can occur from the end of the previous level
j-1
difference estimator to the time when the sliding window begins.
We say a difference estimator
SDiffEst
is active if
\ell\in[a_{j},b_{j}]
for the indices
a_{j}
and
b_{j}
defined in
Algorithm
5
.
The active difference estimators in each level will be the algorithms whose output are subtracted from the initial rough estimate to form the final estimate of
F_{2}(W)
.
The intuition behind the proof of
Lemma
8.6
is that
Lemma
8.5
lower bounds the contributions of the difference estimators of two consecutive blocks.
Hence there cannot be too many active difference estimators in a level or else their contributions will be too large for the level.

Lemma 8.6

(Number of active level
j
difference estimators)
.

Let
i
be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.
Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all subroutines
{\mathcal{A}}
and
SDiffEst
in
Algorithm
5
succeed.
For each
j\in[\beta]
, let
a_{j}
be the smallest index such that
t_{i,j,a_{j}}\geq c_{j-1}
and let
b_{j}
be the largest index such that
t_{i,j,b_{j}}\leq m-W+1
.
Then conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have
b_{j}-a_{j}\leq 512
.

Proof.

Conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have that for each
j\in[\beta]
and all
\ell
,

2^{-j-8}\cdot F(W)<F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+2},t),

by
Lemma
8.5
.
By
Lemma
8.3
,
F_{p}(t_{i},m)\leq 2F_{p}(W)
.
Thus by a telescoping argument, we have that for
j=1
,
a_{j}-b_{j}<512
.

For
j>1
, suppose by way of contradiction that
b_{j}-a_{j}\geq 512
.
Let
k=b_{j}-a_{j}
and for
x\in[k]
, let
u_{x}
be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time
a_{j}+{x-1}
to
a_{j}+x
.
Let
u
be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time
t_{i,j,k}
to
m
and
v
be the frequency vector induced by the updates from
t_{i}
to
m
.
By
Lemma
8.5
,

F\left(u+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}u_{\ell}\right)-F(u)\geq 8\cdot 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(v),

since there are at least
8
disjoint pairs of tuples
(i,j,\ell)
and
(i,j,\ell+1)
if
k>512
.

Thus if
b_{j}-a_{j}>512
, then the sum of the outputs of the active difference estimators at level
j
is more than
8\cdot 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(v)
.
In particular since
t^{(k)}_{i,j,a_{j}}\geq c_{j-1}
, the sum of the outputs of the active difference estimators at level
j
after
c_{j-1}
is at least
8\cdot 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(v)
.
However, by
Lemma
8.5
, each difference estimator at level
j-1
has output at least
2^{-j-7}\cdot F(u)>8\cdot 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(v)
.
Specifically, the difference estimator from times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,c_{j-1}}
to
t^{(k)}_{i,j,c_{j}}
must have output at least
2^{-j-7}\cdot F(u)>8\cdot 2^{-j-8}\cdot F(v)
.
Therefore, there exists some other
z>c_{j-1}
such that
t^{(k)}_{i,j-1,z}\leq m-W+1
, which contradicts the maximality of
c_{j-1}
at level
j-1
.
∎

We next upper bound the value of each difference estimator.
This is important for upper bounding the error associated with the sliding window possibly not beginning exactly where a difference estimator begins.
For example, if a difference estimator exactly computes
F(1:10)-F(4:10)
but the sliding window consists of the last eight updates, i.e.,
F(3:10)
, then the error of our estimator is still only lower bounded by the difference.
Thus it is crucial to not only upper bound the error of the difference estimator, but also the value of the difference estimator.
The intuition behind the proof of
Lemma
8.7
is that the subroutine
MergeSW
only creates a new block for each difference estimator when the contribution is in a particular range.
Specifically, the contribution cannot be too large or else a new block would have already been formed.
Then by smoothness of the function, the contribution of difference estimator cannot grow to be too large regardless of the subsequent updates in the stream.

Lemma 8.7

(Geometric upper bounds on splitting times)
.

Let
i
be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.
Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)
.
Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all subroutines
{\mathcal{A}}
and
SDiffEst
in
Algorithm
5
succeed.
Then conditioned on
{\mathcal{E}}
, we have that for each
j\in[\beta]
and each
\ell
that either
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}=t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}+1
or
F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1},t)\leq 2^{-j/q-7/q}\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1},t)
.

Proof.

Suppose
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}\neq t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}+1
.
Then at some time
x
, the timestamp
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}+1
must have been merged by subroutine
MergeSW
.
Thus

{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell^{\prime}},x,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)+{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell^{\prime}},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1},x,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)\leq 2^{-j-10}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}_{i}(t_{i},x,\eta,\delta).

Let
u_{1}
be frequency vector representing the updates from time
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}
to
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell^{\prime}}-1
,
u_{2}
represent times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell^{\prime}}
to
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}
, and
u
represent times
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell}
to
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}
, so that
u_{1}+u_{2}=u
.
Let
x_{1}
represent the updates between time
t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell+1}
and
x
and let
x_{2}
represent the updates between time
x+1
and
t
.
Since the difference estimators are well-defined by
Lemma
8.4
, we have that
{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell},t^{(k)}_{i,j,\ell^{\prime}},x,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
is at most a
2
-approximation to
F(u_{1}+u_{2}+x_{1})-F(u_{2}+x_{1})
and we similarly obtain a
2
-approximation to
F(u_{2}+x_{1})-F(x_{1})
.
Thus,

F(u+x_{1})-F(x_{1})=F(u_{1}+u_{2}+x_{1})-F(x_{1})\leq 2^{-j-7}\cdot F(x_{1}).

By the
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{q})
smoothness of
F
, we thus have that for any vector
x_{2}
,

F(u+v)-F(v)=F(u+x_{1}+x_{2})-F(x_{1}+x_{2})\leq 2^{-j/q-7/q}\cdot F(x_{1}+x_{2})=2^{-j/q-7/q}\cdot F_{p}(v).

∎

Finally, we show the correctness of
Algorithm
5
.
The main intuition behind
Lemma
8.8
is that there are two sources of error.
The first source of error originates from the boundaries of the blocks corresponding to the difference estimator not aligning with the beginning of the sliding window.
This error, resulting from no difference estimator being assigned to compute the exactly correct value, corresponds to the elements marked by blue in
Figure
5
and cannot be accounted for even if all difference estimators have zero error.
On the other hand, this error is upper bounded by the contribution of a difference estimator at the bottom level, which is bounded by
Lemma
8.7
.
The second source of error stems from the approximation error caused by each of the difference estimators.
Since
Lemma
8.6
bounds the total number of difference estimators being used in our output, we can also upper bound the total approximation error due to the difference estimators.

Lemma 8.8

(Correctness of framework)
.

With high probability,
Algorithm
5
gives a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to the value of
F(W)
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be the event that all algorithms throughout the stream succeed and note that
\mathbf{Pr}\left[{\mathcal{E}}\right]\geq 1-\frac{1}{\poly(n)}
since
\delta=\frac{1}{\poly(m)}
.
Thus we condition on
{\mathcal{E}}
throughout the remainder of the proof.

Let
f
be the frequency vector induced by the window and let
i
be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
and let
c_{0}=t_{i}
.
Let
u
be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time
t_{i}
to
m
.
Let
k
be the smallest integer such that
n^{C}/2^{k}\leq{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},m,1,\delta)
.
For each
j\in[\beta]
, let
a_{j}
be the smallest index such that
t^{(k)}_{i,j,a_{j}}\geq c_{i-1}
and let
b_{j}
be the largest index such that
t^{(k)}_{i,j,b_{j}}\leq m-W+1
.
Note that by construction of the
GuessAndUpdate
forming a new timestamp for all levels
\ell>k
each time a new timestamp at level
k
is formed, then it follows that
t_{i,j,a_{j}}=c_{i-1}
.
Let
u_{j}
be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from time
a_{j}
to
b_{j}
.
Let
v
be the frequency vector induced by the updates of the stream from
c_{\beta}
to
m-W+1
.
Thus, we have
u=\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}u_{j}+v+f
, so it remains to show that:

(1)

F(v+f)-F(f)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)

(2)

We have an additive
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
approximation to
F(v+f)
.

Combined, these two statements show that we have a multiplicative
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of
F_{p}(f)
.

To show that
F(v+f)-F(f)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
, let
\ell
be the index such that
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell}=c_{\beta}
, so that
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell}\leq m-W+1\leq t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1}
.
Note that if
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell}=m-W+1
then
v=0
and so the claim is trivially true.
Otherwise by the definition of the choice of
c_{\beta}
, we have that
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1}>m-W+1
so that
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1}>t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell}+1
.

By
Lemma
8.7
, it follows that
F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell},m)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1},m)\leq 2^{-\beta/q}\cdot F_{p}(t_{i,\beta,k+1},m)
.
Since
\beta={\left\lceil\log\frac{100\cdot 4^{q}}{\varepsilon^{q}}\right\rceil}
, then it follows that
F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell},m)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1},m)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1},m)
.
By
Lemma
8.3
, we have that
\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1},m)\leq 2F(W)=2F(f)
.
Thus,
F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell},m)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1},m)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
.
Since the updates from the times between
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell}
and
m
form the vector
v+f
, we have

F(v+f)-F(t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1},m)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
.
Since
t^{(k)}_{i,\beta,\ell+1}>m-W+1
then by the monotonicity of
F
, we have that
F(v+f)-F(f)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
, as desired.

We next show that we have an additive
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
approximation to
F(v+f)
.
If we define

\Delta_{j}:=F\left(u-\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}u_{k}\right)-F\left(u-\sum_{k=1}^{j}u_{k}\right),

then we have

\sum_{j=1}^{\beta}\Delta_{j}=F(u)-F(v+f).

Observe that
\Delta_{j}
is approximated by the active level
j
difference estimators.
By
Lemma
8.6
, there are at most
512
active indices at level
j
.
Since the difference estimators are well-defined by
Lemma
8.4
and each difference estimator
SDiffEst
uses accuracy parameter
\eta=\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{20}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}
, then the additive error in the estimation of
F(v+f)
incurred by
Y_{j}
is at most

512\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{20}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\cdot F(u)=\frac{\varepsilon}{2^{11}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\cdot F(u).

Summing across all
\beta\leq 2^{9}q\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
levels, then the total error in the estimation
Z
of
F(v+f)
across all levels
Y_{j}
with
j\in[\beta]
is at most

\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\cdot F(u)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f).

Thus, we have an additive
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
approximation to
F(v+f)
as desired.
Since
F(v+f)-F(f)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot F(f)
, then
Algorithm
5
outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F(W)
.
∎

Theorem 8.9

(Framework for sliding window algorithms)
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
be constants and
F
be a monotonic and polynomially bounded function that is
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{q})
-smooth for some constant
q\geq 0
.
Suppose there exists a
(\gamma,\varepsilon,\delta)
-suffix pivoted difference estimator that uses space
\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}S_{F}(m,\delta,\varepsilon)
and a streaming algorithm for
F
that uses space
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}S_{F}(m,\delta,\varepsilon)
, where
S_{F}
is a monotonic function in
m
,
\frac{1}{\delta}
, and
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
.
Then there exists a sliding window algorithm that outputs a
(1+\varepsilon)
approximation to
F
that succeeds with constant probability and uses
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{F}(m,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\cdot\poly\left(\log m,\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
space, where
\delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\poly(m)}\right)
.

Proof.

We first observe that correctness follows from
Lemma
8.8
for
\delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\poly(m)}\right)
.
To analyze the space complexity, note that we maintain
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
guesses for the value of
F
at the end of the stream, since
F
is monotonic and polynomially bounded.
For each guess, we maintain
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
granularities whose space complexity in total forms a geometric series that sums to
S_{F}(m,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\cdot\polylog\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
.
Finally, we repeat this procedure for each of the
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
indices
i
corresponding to the timestamps maintained at the top level by the smooth histogram, again because
F
is monotonic and polynomially bounded.
Therefore, the total space used is
\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot S_{F}(m,\delta^{\prime},\varepsilon)\cdot\poly\left(\log m,\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)
.
∎

8.1
Moment Estimation for
p\in(0,2]

To improve
Algorithm
5
for
F_{p}
moment estimation with
p\in(0,2]
, we remove the additional
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
overhead associated with making
\mathcal{O}\left(\log n\right)
guesses for the value of
F
at the end of the stream.
Instead, we note that since we maintain a constant factor approximation to the value of
F_{p}
due to the smooth histogram, it suffices to partition the substream into blocks for the difference estimators based on the ratio of the difference to the constant factor approximation to the value of
F_{p}
.
We also recall the following useful characterization of the smoothness of the
F_{p}
moment function.

Lemma 8.10
.

[
BO07
]

The
F_{p}
function is
\left(\varepsilon,\frac{\varepsilon^{p}}{p}\right)
-smooth for
p\geq 1
and
(\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
-smooth for
0<p\leq 1
.

Moreover, we require constructions of suffix-pivoted difference estimators for
F_{p}
moment estimation with
p\in(0,2]
.
However, we claim that the previous constructions, i.e., the fixed-prefix difference estimator based on Li’s geometric estimator for
p\in(0,2)
and the fixed-prefix difference estimator based on the inner product sketch for
p=2
are already valid suffix-pivoted difference estimators.
This is because the key property to approximating the difference
F_{p}(v+u_{1})-F_{p}(u_{1})
in “small” space is that
F_{p}(v)
is small.
We can express the variance of both Li’s geometric estimator and the inner product sketch in terms of
F_{p}(v)
so that smaller values of
F_{p}(v)
correspond to smaller variance for the estimators.
Hence even if
F_{p}(v+u_{2})-F_{p}(u_{2})
is much larger than
F_{p}(v+u_{1})-F_{p}(u_{1})
for some
u_{2}\succeq u_{1}
, as long as
F_{p}(v+u_{1})-F_{p}(u_{1})\leq\gamma\cdot F_{p}(v+u_{1})
, then
F_{p}(v)\leq\gamma\cdot F_{p}(v+u_{1})\leq F_{p}(v+u_{2})
.
Therefore, the variance for our difference estimators is at most
\gamma F_{p}(v+u_{2})^{2}
, so we only need to run
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
independent copies of the difference estimators.

The remaining aspects of our algorithms are the same as
Algorithm
5
; for completeness, we give the modified algorithms in
Algorithm
9
.

Algorithm 9

Moment Estimation in the Sliding Window Model

1:

Stream
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}\in[n]
of updates to coordinates of an underlying frequency vector, accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, window parameter
W>0

2:

Robust
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation to
F_{p}

3:

\delta\leftarrow\frac{1}{\poly(n,m)}
,
\eta\leftarrow\frac{\varepsilon}{1024\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}
,
\varphi\leftarrow\sqrt{2}
be a parameter

4:

\beta\leftarrow{\left\lceil\log\frac{100\max(p,1)}{\varepsilon^{\max(p,1)}}\right\rceil}
,
\gamma_{j}\leftarrow 2^{3-j}
for all
j\in[\beta]

5:

for
each update
u_{t}\in[n]
,
t\in[m]

do

6:

UpdateSW

\triangleright
Create new subroutines for each update

7:

MergeSW

\triangleright
Removes extraneous subroutines

8:

return

Z\leftarrow{\textsc{StitchSW}}

\triangleright
Estimate
F_{p}
on sliding window

Algorithm 10

Subroutine
UpdateSW
for moment estimation

1:

Let
s
be the number of instances of
{\mathcal{A}}
.

2:

t_{s+1}\leftarrow t

3:

Start a new instance
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{s+1},t,\eta,\delta)
.

4:

Start a new instance
{\textsc{HHEst}}(t_{s+1},t,\eta,\delta)
.

for

do

\triangleright
Start instances of each granularity

{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t_{s+1},t_{s+1,j,1},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
.

8:

Start a new instance
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{s+1,j,0},t_{s+1,j,1},1,\delta)
.

Algorithm 11

Subroutine
MergeSW
for moment estimation with
p\in[1,2]

1:

Let
s
be the number of instances of
{\mathcal{A}}
and
p\in[1,2]
.

2:

\beta\leftarrow{\left\lceil\log\frac{100p}{\varepsilon^{p}}\right\rceil}

3:

for

i\in[s]
,
j\in[\beta]

do

\triangleright
Difference estimator maintenance

4:

Let

be the number of times

for

do

\triangleright
Merges two algorithms with “small” contributions

6:

if

{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i,j,k-1},t_{i,j,k+1},1,\delta)\leq 2^{-j-10}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},t,\eta,\delta)

then

7:

if

t_{i,j,k}\notin\{t_{i,j-1,*}\}

then

8:

Merge (add) the sketches for
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i,j,k-1},t_{i,j,k},1,\delta)
and
{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i,j,k},t_{i,j,k+1},1,\delta)
.

Merge (add) the sketches for
{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t_{i,j,k-1},t_{i,j,k},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
and
{\textsc{SDiffEst}}(t_{i,j,k},t_{i,j,k+1},t,\gamma_{j},\eta,\delta)
.

10:

Relabel the times
t_{i,j,*}
.

11:

for

i\in[s-2]

do

\triangleright
Smooth histogram maintenance

12:

if

{\mathcal{A}}(t_{i+2},t,\eta,\delta)\geq(1-1/4^{q}){\mathcal{A}}(t_{i},t,\eta,\delta)

then

for

do

Append the times
t_{i+1,j,*}
to
\{t_{i,j,*}\}
.

15:

Delete
t_{i+1}
and all times

.

16:

Relabel the times
\{t_{i}\}
and
\{t_{i,j,*}\}
.

of

5
for moment estimation

1:

Let

be the largest index such that
t_{i}\leq m-W+1
.

2:

4:

for

do

Let

be the smallest index such that

6:

Let
